ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION FOR
FINITE-GROUP EXTENSIONS OF SHIFTS OF FINITE
TYPE
HAOBO MA
Abstract. For a mixing subshift of finite type carrying a finite-group
cocycle, we study how finite-dimensional twisted determinants determine primitive orbit data by conjugacy class. Periodic labels record powers of primitive labels, so passing from periodic determinant logarithms
to primitive label series requires Adams operations g 7→ g m together
with Möbius inversion. This yields an explicit Peter–Weyl determinant
formula for the primitive channels. Under the strict twisted spectral
gap η < λ, the channels have Perron-point values and give class-indexed
Mertens constants. The Frobenius-class Euler-product asymptotic form,
including the exponent |C|/|G|, is classical; the new finite-state content
here is the Adams-corrected determinant formula for the constant. After
scalar finite-part normalisation, the non-trivial coordinates of that constant are recovered from the fixed-label Euler transform Fϱ , not from
the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ . We prove determinant rigidity for
these constants and give a finite S3 witness, with explicit values Fε (1/2)
and Fρ (1/2), showing that naive Artin substitution fails at the productconstant level. Scalar finite-part and canonical cyclic-lift calculations
are retained only as finite-matrix normalisation and comparison tools.

1. Introduction
The problem addressed in this paper is primitive orbit reconstruction for
+
finite-group extensions of mixing subshifts of finite type. Let (XA
, σ) be a
mixing subshift with primitive adjacency matrix A, let λ = ρ(A), and let τ :
EA → G be a cocycle into a finite group. For every irreducible representation
ϱ of G, the twisted transition matrix Aϱ records periodic labels through its
traces. These traces are periodic data. The primitive labels are subtler:
when a primitive orbit with label g is repeated m times, its label becomes
g m . Thus primitive extraction is not ordinary Möbius inversion in the nonabelian setting; it must be coupled to the Adams operations ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m )
on class functions.
The centre of the paper is the Adams–Möbius passage from twisted dynamical determinants to primitive Peter–Weyl channels and then to the
constants in Frobenius-class Euler products. The classical Frobenius-class
Mertens asymptotic supplies the exponent |C|/|G|:
PC (N ) = KC N −|C|/|G| (1 + o(1)).
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. 37B10, 37D35, 11M41, 20C15.
Key words and phrases. Dynamical ζ-function, subshift of finite type, Adams operation,
primitive orbit, finite-group extension, finite-part constant.

The finite-state problem addressed here is the determinant computation and
rigidity of the constant KC after that classical asymptotic layer has been
separated. Under the strict twisted gap its non-trivial Fourier coordinates
are given by the fixed-label Euler transform
Fϱ (λ−1 ) =

not by the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ . Replacing Fϱ by Lϱ is already false
in the finite S3 model of Appendix A. The contribution is therefore the
Adams-corrected fixed-label determinant formula for KC , its finite recovery,
and the obstruction to Artin substitution; it is not a new claim about the
classical class-Mertens exponent.
The finite-group argument has three steps: Main Theorem I linearises
twisted determinants and performs primitive Adams–Möbius inversion;
Main Theorem II evaluates the resulting non-trivial primitive series at
z = λ−1 , after imposing the twisted spectral gap η < λ, and obtains
conjugacy-class Mertens constants; Main Theorem III identifies the
Euler-product constants, while the Fourier recovery of the corresponding
Perron-point primitive channels is a structural corollary. The recovery
of these constants is the Hermitian character-orthogonality formula. Its
forward class coefficient is χ∗ϱ (C) := χϱ (C); for unitary characters this is
equivalently χϱ (C −1 ).
The scalar finite part of ζA and the cyclic root-of-unity layer have narrower
roles. The finite part supplies only the trivial-channel normalisation M(A),
while root-of-unity sampling belongs only to the canonical cyclic lift, where
every edge is labelled by the generator of Z/qZ. It is not the same as taking
arbitrary cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle. The logical direction of the
proofs is

Here the Adams–Möbius arrow is the primitive extraction step; the last arrow is ordinary character orthogonality after the Perron-point values have
been constructed. The formal input–output statements and the scalar normalisation are collected in the next subsection.
The formal theorem statements below summarize this argument before
the technical development begins. The proof spine is short: determinant
traces give periodic Peter–Weyl channels; Adams–Möbius inversion extracts
fixed primitive labels; the strict twisted gap permits evaluation at z = λ−1 ;
and character orthogonality converts those values into class Mertens and
class-product constants. The finite-enclosure statements are used in the
main text only in this minimal sense: they prove that the branch-compatible
fixed-label determinant vector, and hence the S3 witness and the constants
KC , are computable from finite determinant and character data. The fuller
certificate records are kept in the appendix as supporting reproducibility
material, not as an additional theoretical layer. The later rigidity, quotient,

cyclic-detection, and finite-verification statements serve this chain by checking hypotheses or computing its ingredients in finite models; in particular,
the eta-gap obstruction is used only to verify the strict Perron-point input of
Main Theorems II–III, not to classify all finite-group cocycles with a strict
gap. The scalar dynamical ζ-function

finite-part and cyclic-lift material therefore supplies scalar normalisation and
comparison data, not an alternative source of non-abelian primitive information.

For a conjugacy class C ⊂ G, let pn,C denote the number of primitive orbits
of length n whose cocycle label lies in C. The scalar orbit-Mertens constant
M(A) is the trivial-channel constant defined in Definition 2.7; the finitematrix formula behind this normalisation is the calculation in Lemma 6.1.
1.2. Contributions and scope. The paper solves the following finite-state
problem. For a fixed matrix extension (A, τ, G), determine from the twisted
determinant family {det(I − zAϱ ) : ϱ ∈ Irr(G)} the primitive Peter–Weyl
coordinates at the Perron point and hence the constants in conjugacy-class
Mertens sums and Frobenius-class Euler products.
The main results are organized as follows.
(i) Main Theorem I gives the finite Adams–Möbius inversion formula that
recovers the primitive series Πϕ (z) from the determinant logarithms
on |z| < λ−1 . The Adams operation ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ) is forced by the
repetition rule for primitive labels.
(ii) Main Theorem II assumes the strict twisted gap η < λ and evaluates
the non-trivial primitive channels at z = λ−1 , giving the Perron-point
class-Mertens constants by character orthogonality.
(iii) Main Theorem III proves the Adams-corrected Frobenius-class product formula. After the classical exponent |C|/|G| has been separated,
the non-trivial coordinates of the product constant are the fixed-label

The novelty is therefore at the constant level, not at the leading
Frobenius-density exponent. The exponent |C|/|G| belongs to the classical
Chebotarev–Mertens orbit-counting picture; the contribution here is the
determinant recovery of the fixed-label Euler vector, the Adams-corrected
formula for KC , and the finite obstruction to replacing that vector by an
Artin-periodic logarithm. The local comparison in Proposition 3.38 and the
product comparison in Proposition 3.51 make this boundary explicit.
The remaining sections supply the finite normalisations and examples
needed for this chain. The scalar finite part fixes the trivial-channel normalization, the fixed-matrix cyclic-lift discussion gives a comparison boundary,
the quotient and shadow statements identify which coordinates are visible
through abelian data, the eta-gap tests verify the Perron-point hypothesis in
finite examples, and the S3 calculation gives a concrete Artin-substitution obstruction. Main Theorems I–III use these ingredients only in the finite forms
stated below; they do not rely on Hölder/Ruelle transfer theory, completedzeta or ξ-type constructions, secondary spectral-edge asymptotics, scalar
recovery from cyclic lifts, or a general verification theory.

the Frobenius-class Euler product and gives the Adams-corrected constant

Remark 1.2 (Finite-state dependencies). The proofs of Main Theorems I–III
depend on the primitive matrix A, the finite group G, the twisted matrices
Aϱ , the character table and Adams coefficients of G, the Perron root λ,
and the strict gap η < λ. Main Theorem I is assembled in Proposition 3.1;
Main Theorem II is Theorems 3.33 and 3.72; and Main Theorem III is Theorems 3.39 and 3.43 and proposition 3.58. These cited results use finite
matrices, finite character theory, Adams–Möbius inversion, and the Abel
evaluation allowed by ρ(Aϱ ) < λ. In particular, the determinant constants
proved here do not require a Banach-space transfer operator, a thermodynamic limiting theorem, completed-zeta constructions, secondary spectraledge asymptotics, scalar recovery from cyclic lifts, or a general strict-gap
criterion. The finite rational records later in the paper are used as reproducible witnesses for the determinant-constant obstruction.
Proof references. Main Theorem I is proved in Proposition 3.1; Main Theorem II in Theorems 3.33 and 3.72; and Main Theorem III in Theorems 3.39
and 3.43 and proposition 3.58.
Under the hypotheses of Main Theorem III the class product has the form
PC (N ) = KC N −|C|/|G| (1 + O(N −1 ))
for every conjugacy class C ⊂ G. The exponent |C|/|G| is the Frobeniusdensity term inherited from the classical orbit-counting theory. The finitestate contribution of this paper is the non-scalar constant coordinate

The scalar finite part of ζA , the cyclic-lift comparison calculations, and the
rational interval checks in the appendices play auxiliary roles. The scalar calculation supplies the trivial-channel value M(A), while the canonical cyclic
lift is a fixed-matrix comparison model. A robustness lemma in Proposition 3.64 shows that compensated lower-order tails vanish in the constants
used by Main Theorems II–III. Thus the proofs below use finite-matrix determinant identities, finite-group character orthogonality, Adams–Möbius
inversion, and Perron–Frobenius theory for finite non-negative matrices.

channels by Adams–Möbius inversion. Main Theorem II evaluates the resulting non-trivial primitive channels at the Perron point under η < λ. Main

The factor |C|/|G| multiplies the entire Hermitian class-indicator bracket, including the non-trivial fixed-label sum. Each Fϱ (λ−1 ) is computable from the
same finite twisted determinant data as Main Theorem I by substituting the
Adams–Möbius determinant formula into the displayed series. Thus the leading exponent and the existence of such Frobenius-class products are the classical part of the statement; the new assertion is the primitive-label fixed-label
Euler determinant recovery of KC and the resulting obstruction to replacing
Fϱ by the periodic Artin logarithm. As a downstream structural refinement,
the non-trivial Peter–Weyl boundary is a rank-sharp coordinate record only
for the scalar-normalised product constants in fixed branch-compatible determinant coordinates: Corollary 3.47 proves that every real linear record,
after the scalar baseline is fixed, determining all branch-compatible product constants has rank at least the number of non-trivial conjugacy-class
degrees of freedom, and that the Adams-corrected determinant coordinates
attain this bound. It is not asserted as a universal inverse-observation theorem for arbitrary data from the extension, nor as a branch-free determinant
statement. This distinction is part of the statement: the exponent |C|/|G|
and the existence of Frobenius-class Mertens products belong to the Parry–
Pollicott, Noorani–Parry, Sharp, and Mesliza–Noorani orbit-counting lineage cited above; the novelty asserted here begins only after that classical
leading term has been separated, namely at the Adams–Möbius fixed-label
determinant formula for the product constant.
The proof path is the following dependency roadmap; every arrow is proved
in the indicated body result, and the rigidity row is only the scalar-normalised,
branch-compatible determinant-coordinate rigidity used to evaluate the constants.

Proof. This front-matter theorem is a synopsis of the body theorem chain,
and its proof is located in the following body results. The fixed-label Euler
transform is established in Theorem 3.11; applying it class by class gives the
product asymptotic and the displayed expression for KC in Theorem 3.39.
The claim that every value Fϱ (λ−1 ), and hence every KC , is recovered from
the finite twisted determinant data is proved in Propositions 3.58 and 3.60,
theorem 3.43, and corollary 3.45. The scalar-normalised coordinate and
rank statements are precisely Corollary 3.47, while the comparison with
the periodic Artin logarithm is Proposition 3.51 and theorem 3.66. Thus
the proof uses only the chain cited in the statement; the finite S3 rational
bounds enter later only as explicit witnesses to the obstruction and not as
hypotheses for the product theorem.
□
The product constants are unaffected by auxiliary lower-order finite tails:
Proposition 3.64 records that any such refinement whose compensated
tails vanish leaves MC (A, τ ; G), KC , and the recovered values Fϱ (λ−1 )
unchanged. It is a robustness statement for the main constants, not a
fourth main theorem or an alternative proof of the product constants.
The corollary-level refinements Corollaries 3.47 and 3.67 then assemble
the already proved constants into the fixed finite determinant-coordinate
Peter–Weyl vector and a Parseval obstruction bound. They are used
below only as structural consequences and finite witnesses for the scalarnormalised product constants in that branch-compatible chart, not as
observation-system minimality claims or as additional parts of Main
Theorem III.

the determinant linearisation and Adams–Möbius primitive inversion are

Theorem 3.7 and corollary 3.10. The determinant-germ formula and coefficient forms are Corollary 3.18 and theorems 3.20 and 3.23. Main Theorem II
is proved by Theorems 3.33 and 3.72 and proposition 3.70. Main Theorem III
is Theorem 3.39. The determinant evaluation in Proposition 3.58 and corollary 3.42 identifies the constant without adding hypotheses to the main theorems. The eta-gap criterion Theorem 4.1 explains when the strict-gap hypothesis fails for the fixed finite cocycle; its one-dimensional loop-character
and perfect-loop-group consequences are Corollaries 4.3 and 4.5. These results certify the Perron-point hypothesis but are not separate mechanisms
for product constants or a classification of all strict-gap cocycles.
The only retained secondary-tail interface is the robustness lemma Proposition 3.64: once the determinant coordinates and product constants have
been fixed by the main chain, compensated finite tails with zero Abel contribution add no Perron-boundary coordinate, Mertens constant, or Eulerproduct constant.

the Peter–Weyl decomposition splits the Perron-point class profile into its
one-dimensional sector and its higher-dimensional sector. The span of all
cyclic quotient observables is exactly the one-dimensional sector; therefore
its projection is the abelian shadow, orthogonal to the genuine non-abelian
defect. Under η < λ, the full primitive class profile is generated by cyclic
quotient shadows exactly when every higher-dimensional primitive channel
Πϱ (λ−1 ) vanishes. These assertions are proved in Theorems 5.4, 5.8 and 5.10
and corollary 5.5.
Similarly, a scalar record formed only from the fixed matrix A, such
as FP(A), M(A), the reduced polynomial FA , the branch-lifted cyclic-lift
record {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , or a torsion layer {FA (ωqj )}0≤j<q , cannot by itself recover the non-trivial finite-group profile for a family of cocycles over the
same base matrix. The precise projection boundary is Theorem 5.2: scalar
data determine a single normalised class profile across such a family exactly
when all non-trivial Perron-point channels Πτϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1, are already independent of τ . Thus the scalar finite-part and cyclic-lift statements below
are fixed-matrix inputs and do not enlarge the finite-group determinant and
product results; the two-cocycle separation is Corollary 5.3.
An explicit S3 -extension separates the two obstruction types at the
level relevant to Main Theorem III. Its fixed-label Euler-transform bounds
Fε (1/2) ∈ (−381/1000, −380/1000) and Fρ (1/2) ∈ (−923/1000, −922/1000)
force the product-constant obstruction to naive Artin substitution. The
accompanying primitive bounds show that the sign-character trace vanishes
but its primitive value is non-zero by the quadratic Adams mechanism,
while the standard two-dimensional channel gives the rationally verified
higher-dimensional cyclic detection defect. This is a finite-state obstruction
model, not a new periodic-orbit distribution theorem.

Under the twisted spectral gap, the body proves the quotient-shadow description of the normalised primitive class profile in Theorems 5.4 and 5.8,
corollary 5.5, and lemmas 5.7 and 5.9. It includes scalar projection onto the
one-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector, functoriality for finite quotients, the
orthogonal decomposition into abelian shadow and genuine non-abelian defect, and exact detection of the one-dimensional sector by cyclic quotients of
the fixed cocycle. The root-of-unity determinant layers log det(I − ωqj zA)−1
occur only in the separate canonical cyclic-lift comparison model, not as
invariants of arbitrary cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle.

At the Perron point, the cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle detect exactly the
one-dimensional Peter–Weyl shadow of the normalised primitive class profile,
and the orthogonal complement is the non-abelian defect. The scalar rootof-unity determinant layers log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 belong only to the canonical
cyclic-lift comparison model and do not give invariants of arbitrary cyclic
quotients of τ . The scalar finite part contributes only the trivial-channel
normalisation M(A) = γ + FP(A).
Proof. The two displayed pullback identities are precisely the determinant
and primitive-channel functoriality of Theorem 5.4. The cyclic detection assertion follows by combining the cyclic-quotient readout of Corollary 5.5 with
the character-theoretic separation in Lemma 5.9; the complementary higherdimensional component is the non-abelian defect of Theorem 5.8. The distinction between inherited cyclic quotients of τ and the scalar root-of-unity
layers of the canonical cyclic lift is exactly Proposition 5.6 and lemma 5.7.
Finally, the trivial-channel scalar normalisation is Lemma 6.1 and corollary 6.3.
□
The second scalar component is the separate fixed-matrix lift model, related to inverse spectral questions for SFTs [21]. In this article we use only
the finite-dimensional normalisation and comparison boundary supplied by
that model. Branch-lifted reconstruction and one-layer Fourier recovery belong to separate inverse problems; here they serve only to distinguish scalar
cyclic-lift records from non-abelian class-profile invariants. The terminology
of cyclic, torsion, and exponential spectral records is fixed in Definition 2.8.
1.4. Relation to existing work. The natural comparison class is the
symbolic-dynamics and dynamical ζ-function literature on prime cycles, finite extensions, and twisted determinant factorizations. In that literature

12

HAOBO MA

the leading Chebotarev distribution and Frobenius-class product asymptotics are classical inputs. Parry–Pollicott’s Chebotarev theorem for Axiom A Galois coverings [31], Noorani–Parry’s finite homogeneous-extension
theorem for shifts [27], Sharp’s Mertens theorem for Axiom A flows [41], and
Mesliza–Noorani’s Frobenius-class Mertens product theorem [24] supply the
background against which Main Theorems II–III should be read. The exponent |C|/|G| and the existence of Frobenius-class products are therefore not
claimed as new here. The theorem-local contribution is the finite Adams–
Möbius determinant recovery of the class constants after that classical leading term has been separated.
At the determinant level, the paper is closer to Fried’s twisted periodic
point formulae [13, 12], the twisted Perron–Frobenius framework of Adachi–
Sunada [1], and graph-Artin determinant theories of Hashimoto, Sunada,
Stark–Terras, and related finite-graph covering work [14, 15, 16, 47, 44,
45, 46]. Those works encode periodic or prime-cycle data by determinants
or representation-theoretic L-functions. The present finite-state calculation
fixes one finite-group SFT cocycle and asks the inverse question needed for
the constants: from the full family of twisted determinants, recover the primitive Peter–Weyl channels. The extra operation is the finite-group Adams
correction ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ), forced by repetitions of a primitive orbit.
Finite group actions and extensions of SFTs form the adjacent symbolic
setting. Adler–Kitchens–Marcus [2], Fiebig [11], Silver–Williams [43], Boyle–
Schmieding [7], Boyle–Carlsen–Eilers [6], and Matsumoto [23] treat finite
actions, quotients, periodic data, and classification or obstruction questions.
Here the extension is fixed; the issue is not existence or flow-equivalence
classification but determinant-level recovery of Πϱ (λ−1 ), MC (A, τ ; G), and
the Adams-corrected Euler-product constants.
Recent resonance results, including Humbert’s first-Ruelle-resonance theorem for Anosov flows with smooth potentials [17], are comparison background for the analytic transfer-operator landscape. They are not hypotheses, consequences, or priority-conflicting predecessors for the finite-state
determinant chain proved here, which uses only finite twisted matrices,
character-table orthogonality, and the stated Perron-point gap η < λ.
The result isolated below is the fixed-label Euler transform
X Πϱ (z r )
,
Fϱ (z) =
r
r≥1
its Adams-corrected determinant expression, and the obstruction to replacing it by the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ in Frobenius-class product constants.
Thus this paper fixes one finite SFT cocycle and proves the determinant recovery of primitive Peter–Weyl channels, the Perron-point class Mertens constants, and the Adams-corrected fixed-label Euler-product constants. The
quotient, shadow, eta-gap, and S3 statements later in the paper are supporting corollaries and finite certificates: they explain functoriality, scalar
no-descent, the boundary of the strict twisted gap, and a concrete obstruction to replacing Fϱ by Lϱ . They are not separate classification theorems
competing with Main Theorems I–III.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

13

1.5. Plan of the paper. Section 2 fixes the symbolic, determinant, and
finite-group notation used throughout the article. Section 3 contains the
main proof chain: Adams–Möbius determinant inversion gives the primitive
Peter–Weyl channels, the Perron-point evaluation gives the Frobenius-class
Mertens constants, and the fixed-label Euler transform gives the Adamscorrected product constants. Section 4 records finite criteria for the strict
twisted gap η < λ, the hypothesis that allows the non-trivial channels to
be evaluated at the Perron point. Section 5 describes quotient and abelian
shadow functoriality, and Section 6 isolates the scalar finite part and nodescent boundary needed for the trivial-channel normalization. The canonical cyclic-lift and finite-observation scalar results are used only as fixedmatrix comparison material, not as inputs to the finite-group determinant
inversion.
The appendices provide the finite computations cited by the main text.
The robustness statement Proposition 3.64 shows that lower-order compensated tails do not alter the constants in Main Theorems II–III; refined
secondary-tail asymptotics are left for separate work. The scalar material retained here is the finite part and the projection boundary used by the main
text. The S3 appendices Appendices A and B display the finite matrices
and rational inequalities used to certify the concrete Artin-substitution obstruction in Main Theorem III, while the fixed-label record appendix gives
reproducibility data for these finite determinant constants.
2. Preliminaries
The notation in this section follows the hierarchy of the paper. We first
fix primitive orbit counts for the base shift, then the finite-group extension
and Adams operations used in the main theorem chain. The reduced determinant, finite part, and cyclic-lift notation come afterwards because they
serve only as scalar normalisation and canonical cyclic-lift comparison data.
Finite-data guide. All main objects are finite. The base datum is a
primitive adjacency matrix A of a finite edge shift, with Perron root λ =
ρ(A) > 0. A cocycle τ : EA → G assigns labels in a finite group. For
ϱ ∈ Irr(G), the twisted matrix Aϱ is the finite block transition matrix whose
traces record periodic labels in the ϱ-channel, and
η := max ρ(Aϱ )
ϱ̸=1

is the non-trivial twisted spectral radius, with the empty maximum read as
0. The strict inequality η < λ is an additional finite-matrix hypothesis in
Main Theorems II and III; it is not inferred from Main Theorem I. For a
class function ϕ, Πϕ (z) denotes the primitive fixed-label series, Lϕ (z) the
periodic Artin logarithm, and
X Πϕ (z r )
Fϕ (z) =
r
r≥1
the fixed-label Euler transform. The distinction between Fϕ and Lϕ is the
Adams distinction: repetitions change labels by g 7→ g r . The Adams operation is ψ r ϕ(g) = ϕ(g r ), and the Adams–Möbius coefficients are the finite

14

HAOBO MA

character-table coefficients obtained when each ψ r χπ is expanded in the
irreducible characters.
The Fourier convention is Hermitian. The forward class coefficient is
χ∗ϱ (C) := χϱ (C) = χϱ (C −1 ), while inverse recovery uses χσ (C) without
conjugation. The scalar normalisations are C(A) = det′ (I − A/λ)−1 , FP(A),
and M(A) = γ + FP(A). The trivial determinant det(I − zA) is singular
at z = λ−1 , so the trivial channel is never evaluated there as an ordinary
determinant branch; it enters only through the finite part M(A) and the
reduced constant C(A). Non-trivial channels may be evaluated at z = λ−1
only after the strict gap η < λ has made their determinant branches regular.

2.5. Standing notation. Throughout the paper ωq = e2πi/q denotes a
primitive qth root of unity, and Φq the qth cyclotomic polynomial. We
reserve the notation ϱ for irreducible representations of finite groups and
keep ρ(·) for spectral radii. Sections 3 and 5 use the graph and cocycle
picture for finite-group extensions. The later scalar section, Section 6, uses
only the primitive matrix formalism and cyclic lifts.
3. Adams–Möbius primitive inversion for finite-group
extensions
This section proves the finite-group primitive inversion results. Twisted
matrices Aϱ first linearise periodic data in Peter–Weyl coordinates; the
Adams–Möbius identity then removes repetitions of primitive orbits, where
labels change from g to g m . We use standard character-ring and Adamsoperation formalism as in [4, 40, 19], together with the classical Witt-vector
and necklace formalism for passing from repeated to primitive data [10, 25,
48].
The proof is organised in two labelled blocks and several bridge statements. Only the first block is part of Main Theorem I. The block Lemma 3.6,
theorem 3.7, and corollaries 3.10, 3.18 and 3.21, is the Adams–Möbius determinant and coefficient mechanism and does not use a twisted spectral
gap. The second block, Theorems 3.33 and 3.72 and proposition 3.70, belongs to Main Theorem II and starts only after the twisted spectral gap
η = maxϱ̸=1 ρ(Aϱ ) < λ is imposed and converts the primitive channels
into the class-indexed constants MC (A, τ ; G), from which the channels are
read back by non-abelian character orthogonality. The intervening classdecomposition formulas are bridge statements between these two blocks,
not extra headline theorems.
Throughout the section, the underlying finite-state data are the data fixed
in Proposition 2.5; the additional boundary assumptions λ > 1 and η < λ
are invoked only in the statements where Perron-point values or Mertens
constants are evaluated.
3.1. Main Theorem I: Adams–Möbius primitive determinant
linearisation. The first main block has five steps. The twisted trace
identity converts matrices into periodic Peter–Weyl sums. The determinant
linearisation rewrites those sums as logarithms of twisted determinants.
The Adams–Möbius inversion then removes repetitions of primitive orbits, and the irreducible-channel formula rewrites the result entirely in
terms of the determinant data. Finally, coefficient extraction gives the
primitive Peter–Weyl trace formula. The preliminary class-indicator and
block-diagonalisation propositions below set up this mechanism; they are
not separate main results.
For reference, the logical dependency map for this block is
twisted traces → determinant linearisation
→ Adams–Möbius inversion → irreducible channels
→ class-count recovery.
The next proposition records the assembled finite construction with its hypotheses and outputs, so that the introduction, the Peter–Weyl bridge, and

22

HAOBO MA

the later Mertens section all refer to a single proved statement rather than
to an informal chain of aliases.
Proposition 3.1 (Finite determinant assembly for Main Theorem I). The
following implications assemble Main Theorem I without any use of the
Perron-boundary hypothesis η < λ.
(a) Lemma 3.6 and theorem 3.7 identify, for every class function ϕ, the periodic logarithm Lϕ (z) on |z| < λ−1 as a finite character-table linear combination of the determinant logarithms log det(I − zAπ )−1 , π ∈ Irr(G).
(b) Corollary 3.10 applies the Adams power maps in the character ring to
recover the primitive class series Πϕ (z) from those periodic determinant
logarithms.
(c) Corollary 3.18 and theorem 3.20 specialize the preceding identity to irreducible characters and give, respectively, the determinant-germ formula
and the coefficient formula for every primitive Peter–Weyl channel.
(d) Theorem 3.23 applies finite character orthogonality to those channels
and gives the primitive Frobenius-class counts from the same finite determinant and Adams data.
Consequently Main Theorem I is exactly the finite-state determinant construction consisting of the twisted matrices Aπ , the finite character table of
G, and the power maps on conjugacy classes.
Proof. Part (a) is the content of Lemma 3.6 and theorem 3.7: the trace identity expresses periodic labelled traces
in Peter–Weyl coordinates, and the
P
usual identity log det(I − zAπ )−1 = n≥1 Tr(Anπ )z n /n converts those traces
into determinant logarithms in the open Perron disc. Part (b) is exactly
Corollary 3.10; its proof is Möbius inversion of the primitive expansion with
the label changed by g 7→ g m , hence by the Adams operation ψ m . Part (c)
follows by substituting ϕ = χϱ and then either leaving the identity as a
determinant germ or extracting coefficients. The Adams coefficients are the
(m)
finite inner products aϱ,π of (30), so no analytic input beyond convergence
−1
in |z| < λ is introduced. Part (d) is the orthogonality inversion for the
basis of irreducible characters on class functions, applied coefficient by coefficient. These four steps are precisely the determinant, primitive-channel,
and class-recovery assertions claimed as Main Theorem I.
□

The next lemma is a finite-matrix/Peter–Weyl translation of the twisted
gap into the skew-product peripheral spectrum. It is included as a background checkable criterion for the hypotheses used later, not as a new
dynamical extension criterion; compare the finite homogeneous extension
framework of [26, 27].

(i) η < λ.

which is the displayed spectral decomposition. The trivial representation

Therefore η < λ holds exactly when every non-trivial block Aϱ has spectral
radius strictly less than λ. Since every eigenvalue of Aϱ has modulus at most
ρ(Aϱ ), this is equivalent to saying that every eigenvalue of every non-trivial
block has modulus strictly less than λ, which is statement (ii). Because
the trivial block contributes exactly one peripheral eigenvalue, namely the
simple eigenvalue λ, statement (ii) is equivalent to saying that the peripheral
spectral multiset of Ae consists of that single copy of λ. This is statement

(iii). A mere point-set identity spec(A)
exclude extra copies of λ coming from non-trivial blocks, so multiplicity is
essential. Finally, statement (iii) says precisely that Ae has spectral radius λ,
that λ is algebraically simple, and that every other eigenvalue has modulus
strictly less than λ, which is statement (iv).
Finally, if Ae is irreducible, then Perron–Frobenius theory for irreducible
non-negative matrices says that irreducibility together with the absence of
any peripheral eigenvalue other than the simple Perron eigenvalue is equivalent to primitivity. Thus, under irreducibility, statements (i)–(iv) are also
e
equivalent to primitivity of A.

Up to this point, the material is representation-theoretic infrastructure. The
specific content of the present paper begins when the determinant data are
pushed to the primitive level by Adams–Möbius inversion.
The next lemma fixes the trace convention used throughout the rest of
the section. It is the only place where the twisted matrices are identified
with fixed-point sums; afterwards all primitive formulas are obtained by
class-function expansion and Adams–Möbius inversion. Together with the
determinant linearisation and its primitive corollaries below, it forms Main
Theorem I from the introduction. The point of spelling it out separately
is that the passage from determinants to primitive data has two genuinely
distinct stages: matrix traces first give periodic Peter–Weyl sums, and only
then does Adams–Möbius inversion remove repetitions of primitive orbits.
Thus the identity in Lemma 3.6 is not a primitive-orbit statement; it is the
periodic input on which the primitive inversion operates.

Remark 3.9 (Where ordinary Möbius inversion stops). If ϕ = 1, the factor
ϕ(τ (γ)r ) in (16) is independent of r, and Corollary 3.10 reduces to the
usual scalar Möbius inversion for primitive orbit counts. For a non-trivial
class function this independence fails: the r-fold repetition changes the label
from g to g r . The Adams operator ψ r is exactly the correction needed for
this change. Thus the primitive finite-group step below is not obtained by
applying the scalar formula representation by representation; it uses the
Adams action on the character ring before Möbius inversion can remove
repetitions. For the general “primitive from repetitions” viewpoint, compare
the necklace/Witt formalism in [25, 48].

3.2. Fixed-label Euler transform versus periodic Artin logarithm.
The next theorem is the notational hinge between Main Theorem I and
the class-product constants. The periodic determinant logarithm Lϕ sums
repeated primitive labels as ϕ(τ (γ)r ), while the fixed-label Euler transform
F
keeps the primitive label fixed and then forms the Euler-product tail
Pϕ
r
r≥1 Πϕ (z )/r. Thus Πϕ is the primitive Peter–Weyl channel, Lϕ is the
Artin/periodic logarithm, and Fϕ is the quantity entering the Frobeniusclass product constant. For non-trivial characters its Perron-point value
is always read with the Abel-path branch convention of the determinant
formula below: the scalar Perron determinant is deleted only in the singular
triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1), whereas scalar terms with rm ≥ 2 remain regular
and are retained.
Theorem 3.11 (Fixed-label Euler transform). Let ϕ : G → C be a class
function. For every |z| < λ−1 , the fixed-label Euler transform satisfies

Thus the Artin logarithm Lϕ and the primitive-label Euler transform Fϕ
agree only in channels where the Adams powers do not change the relevant primitive labels. If λ > 1, η < λ, and ϕ is orthogonal to the trivial
character, then Fϕ (λ−1 ) is well defined by absolute convergence. Its determinant continuation at the Perron point is the local determinant evaluation of
Proposition 3.58: the single singular scalar summand (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) is
omitted before any logarithm is evaluated, while scalar components of ψ m ϕ
with rm ≥ 2 remain in the formula and are regular because λ−rm A1 lies
strictly inside the Perron disc. Thus non-trivial characters may acquire trivial components under Adams powers, but only away from the scalar Perron
boundary. The later product-constant role of this fixed-label value, and the
obstruction to substituting Lϕ , are isolated in Proposition 3.38.

Remark 3.15 (Relation to necklace/Witt inversion and twisted-zeta precedents). The cancellation in Corollary 3.10 is the finite-group analogue of
the classical “primitive from repetitions” mechanism in the necklace and
Witt-vector literature [25, 48]. What is specific here is the finite-state cocycle input: repetitions replace a primitive label g by g m , so the relevant
correction is not scalar Möbius inversion alone but the Adams operation
ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ) inside the character ring of one fixed finite-group extension.
Twisted periodic-point formulas and determinant identities also appear in
Fried’s work [13, 12] and in the dynamical-zeta treatment of Parry–Pollicott
[32]. Those works, together with classical dynamical Mertens and Chebotarev product theorems, account for the leading exponent and the existence
scale of the Frobenius-class asymptotic. The claim made here begins after
that classical layer: the finite-state Adams–Möbius primitive inversion identifies the fixed-label determinant constant which enters the product, rather
than the periodic Artin logarithm.

The determinant identity above is the generating-function part of Main Theorem I. The next theorem is the coefficient recovery from the same identity,
while the following corollary is only the data-interface version: the theorem
gives the formula, and the corollary records that the finite twisted trace, or
determinant, data is exactly the input needed to use it. Neither the twisted
spectral gap nor cyclic root-of-unity reconstruction enters this first main
theorem.
Remark 3.19 (Abelian toy model: the cyclic lift). Let G = Z/qZ, and let
χj (h) = ωqjh be its irreducible characters. The canonical cyclic-lift cocycle
is the special cocycle τq : EA → Z/qZ with τq (e) = 1 on every edge; its
skew-product adjacency matrix is A ⊗ Pq . For this cocycle, and not for a
general Z/qZ-valued edge labelling, one has Aχj = ωqj A, and therefore
Lχj (z) = log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 .
The Adams–Möbius inversion (17) then reduces to the ordinary Fourier decomposition on the q-th roots of unity. This is the concrete bridge between
the abelian formulas of Section 6 and the present finite-group formalism, but
only after the original cocycle has been replaced by this canonical cyclic-lift
cocycle. This model is also not an example of the twisted spectral gap used
later when q > 1: for every non-trivial character χj , ρ(Aχj ) = ρ(A) = λ,
so the non-trivial twisted radius is η = λ, not < λ. The cyclic-lift layer
is therefore a finite-matrix comparison case, not a source of Perron-point
non-trivial-channel constants.

(m)

This is the coefficient-level form of Main Theorem I, not a preliminary estimate: it is the point at which the finite twisted determinant family becomes
primitive orbit data. The trace lemma supplies the periodic input, the
linearisation theorem rewrites it as twisted determinant data, the Adams–
Möbius corollary removes repetitions, and the determinant corollary and
trace theorem give the primitive Peter–Weyl series and coefficients.
3.4. Bridge to classes and Main Theorem II. The primitive Peter–
Weyl coefficients determine the conjugacy-class counts by Fourier inversion
on G. The next proposition is a bridge: it translates the primitive channels
of Main Theorem I into class counts, but it does not add a new independent
main theorem.

If G is trivial, this maximum is empty and η = 0. Thus the formal determinant identities Theorems 3.7, 3.20 and 3.23 and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18
are unconditional in the open Perron disc, while the estimates and constants
from Lemma 3.25 through Theorem 3.72 are conditional on λ > 1 and the
displayed gap. The only scalar input used below is the trivial-channel orbitMertens constant M(A); its finite-matrix formula is proved independently
in Section 6.
The class Mertens theorem below is the analytic part of Main Theorem II
from the introduction. The leading density term is included to identify the
constant term and normalisation; the new ingredient in this section is the
passage from the twisted determinant channels to the primitive Perron-point
class profile. The later Fourier formula is a recovery of the constants by
character orthogonality. The proof is assembled from the next five lemmas:
class decomposition, the non-trivial channel estimate, absolute convergence
at the Perron point, a geometric-harmonic tail bound, and the trivial Perronchannel contribution.

3.5. Class Mertens constants and Adams-corrected Euler products.
This subsection is the finite-group Mertens core of the paper. Its opening blocks give the additive constants of Main Theorem II and reduce the
Frobenius-class Euler product to a fixed-label product tail. The middle
blocks evaluate that fixed-label vector by finite twisted determinants and
identify the resulting scalar-normalised product coordinates. The closing
blocks record the rigidity/readout consequences and the Artin-substitution
obstruction. Thus the reader should follow one chain, not several competing
proof tracks:
additive class constants
↓ product-tail reduction
fixed-label Euler transform
↓ finite determinant evaluation
rigidity and readout
↓ comparison
Artin obstruction.
Here Theorem 3.33 supplies the additive constants, while Theorems 3.11
and 3.39 prove Main Theorem III. The remaining results in the subsection
have three roles: finite determinant evaluation Propositions 3.58 and 3.60
and theorem 3.43, coordinate comparison and branch conventions Propositions 3.46 and 3.51 and corollary 3.44, and Artin-substitution obstruction
Theorem 3.66 and corollary 3.67. Thus the subsequent stability, obstruction,
and quotient-shadow statements use the same recovered product vector without introducing further product constants.
The notation and dependencies for Main Theorem III are as follows.

With this notation, the proof blocks are additive class constants Theorems 3.33 and 3.72, product-tail reduction Section 3.5.2, fixed-label Euler transform Section 3.5.3, determinant evaluation Section 3.5.8, productcoordinate rigidity and readout Sections 3.5.5 and 3.5.9, classical comparison
Section 3.5.6, and Artin obstruction certificates Theorem 3.66.
The constant normalisation is therefore visible in one line:
{MC }C ⇐⇒ {Πϱ (λ−1 )}ϱ̸=1 ,

{KC }C + C(A) ⇐⇒ {Fϱ (λ−1 )}ϱ̸=1 .

The first equivalence is the additive class-Mertens formula together with
the Hermitian class transform. The second is the scalar finite-part identity
M(A) = γ + FP(A) = γ + log C(A) combined with the fixed-label Euler
transform and the positive-branch product formula for KC .
For reference, the finite-data closure statement below is placed at the
front because the section intentionally proves several consequences of the
same scalar-normalised vector rather than scattering them into independent
black boxes.
The local proof spine should be read in the following order. First, Theorem 3.33 proves the additive Chebotarev–Mertens asymptotic under the two
headline hypotheses λ > 1 and η < λ. Second, Theorems 3.11 and 3.39 converts those additive constants into the fixed-label Euler-product constants;
this is the only step where the distinction between the fixed-label transform
Fϱ and the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ enters. Third, the determinant and
rigidity results evaluate the non-trivial Perron coordinates from finite determinant data and record the effective truncation convention. Finally, the
comparison, observation-budget, and obstruction results are consequences
of the already recovered product vector. Thus the later rigidity, stability,
and S3 certificate statements do not feed back into the proofs of Main Theorems II–III; they only test or separate the product constants produced by
this ordered chain.

namely λ > 1 and the strict twisted spectral gap η < λ. Let

(i) the first arrow is exactly Main Theorem I together with Theorem 3.33;
it uses coefficient extraction, Adams coefficients, finite character orthogonality, and only the regular Abel-path Perron boundary values of
the non-trivial twisted determinant series;
(ii) the second arrow is the class-Mertens identity (39);
(iii) the third arrow is the fixed-label Euler transform of Theorem 3.11;
(iv) the final equivalence is the scalar-normalised Fourier inversion (78) of
Proposition 3.51, equivalently the rigidity formula of Theorem 3.43.
Consequently the finite determinant data, together with the stated branch and
scalar-normalisation conventions, determines the whole output of Main Theorems II–III in this determinant-coordinate chart. Conversely, once C(A)
is fixed and the same branch-compatible product-coordinate chart is used,
equality of two product-constant vectors (KC )C is equivalent to equality of all
non-trivial fixed-label Perron coordinates (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 . Thus any attempted
substitute for the Adams-corrected product constants is correct for every conjugacy class if and only if it matches the same non-trivial coordinate vector
in this fixed scalar-normalised determinant model.
The source closure is literal. Let (A′ , τ ′ , G) be another finite-group extension satisfying the same hypotheses with the same Perron value λ, the same
scalar normalisation C(A′ ) = C(A), and the same group G. Suppose that
the branch-compatible determinant values which occur in Proposition 3.58
agree for the two systems, namely
Log det(I − λ−k Aπ )−1 = Log det(I − λ−k A′π )−1
for every k = rm ≥ 2 and every π ∈ Irr(G), and also for the Abel-path
non-trivial boundary values with k = 1 and π ̸= 1. Then the two extensions
have identical values
Fϱ (λ−1 ),

MC (A, τ ; G),

KC

for all non-trivial ϱ and all conjugacy classes C. Conversely, if C(A′ ) =
C(A), the same branch-compatible product-coordinate chart is used, and the
positive product vectors (KC )C agree, then all non-trivial fixed-label Perron
coordinates Fϱ (λ−1 ) agree in that chart. Hence auxiliary scalar comparisons,
lower-order tail refinements, or notational substitutes can affect the main
constants only by changing either the scalar normalisation or one of these
non-trivial determinant boundary coordinates.
Proof. Main Theorem I expresses the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient
πn,ϱ (A, τ ) by Adams–Möbius inversion from the finite twisted determinant
logarithms. Finite character orthogonality then recovers every primitive
class count pn,C . Under the strict twisted spectral gap, Theorem 3.33
evaluates the Perron-point primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) for ϱ ̸= 1 along the
specified Abel path and gives the class-Mertens constants by (39). This
proves the first two arrows.
The fixed-label Euler transform is a convergent Adams-weighted transform of the primitive channels at the Perron point; this is precisely Theorem 3.11. Substituting those values into Theorem 3.39 gives the product
constants. After the scalar base C(A) has been separated, the normalised

HAOBO MA

logarithmic vector
K(C) = −

has Fourier expansion K(C) = ϱ̸=1 χ∗ϱ (C)Fϱ (λ−1 ). Orthogonality of the
irreducible characters gives the inverse formula (78). Hence the product
vector and the non-trivial fixed-label coordinate vector determine one another once the scalar normalisation and branch-compatible determinantcoordinate chart are fixed.
The final assertion follows immediately from the same inverse Fourier formula in the fixed chart. If a branch-compatible substitute has all the same
class constants after the same scalar normalisation, its normalised logarithmic vector is the same and therefore all non-trivial Fourier coordinates agree.
If the coordinates agree in that chart, the displayed Fourier expansion gives
the same normalised logarithmic vector and hence the same class constants.
The stability, comparison, and obstruction results later in the subsection use
only these coordinates, so they factor through the stated chain.
It remains to prove the two-extension source-closure assertion. The
(m)
Adams coefficients aϱ,π , the Möbius factors, and the class characters
depend only on the common finite group G. Therefore the determinant
formula of Proposition 3.58 is the same absolutely convergent linear
combination of the displayed branch-compatible determinant values for the
two systems. Equality of those values gives Fϱ (λ−1 ) = Fϱ′ (λ−1 ) for every
ϱ ̸= 1. The same determinant-value hypothesis applies to the boundary
formula of Lemma 3.69, so Πϱ (λ−1 ) = Π′ϱ (λ−1 ) for every non-trivial ϱ. The
class-Mertens formula (39) then gives equality of the additive constants
MC , and the product formula (54), together with C(A′ ) = C(A), gives
equality of all KC .
Conversely, if the scalar normalisations, branch-compatible productcoordinate conventions, and positive product vectors agree, then the
normalised logarithmic vectors K agree. Applying the inverse Fourier
formula (78), or equivalently (100), gives equality of every non-trivial
fixed-label coordinate in that chart. Thus an auxiliary record which leaves
the scalar normalisation and these determinant boundary coordinates
fixed is invisible to the main constants, while changing a main constant
necessarily changes one of those two pieces of data. This proves the stated
source closure.

P

Assembly of the three main statements. Under the hypotheses stated in the
introduction, the three displayed introduction theorems are exactly the following body theorem chain.
(i) Main Theorem I is the finite determinant interface of Proposition 3.1:
twisted traces give the class-function determinant logarithms, Adams–
Möbius inversion gives the primitive channels, and finite character
orthogonality gives the primitive Frobenius-class counts.
(ii) Main Theorem II is the Perron-boundary continuation of those same
primitive channels under λ > 1 and η < λ, namely Theorems 3.33
and 3.72 and proposition 3.70. It produces the class constants
MC (A, τ ; G) and their Hermitian Fourier–Parseval readout.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

47

(iii) Main Theorem III is the fixed-label Euler-product evaluation and rigidity statement Theorems 3.39, 3.43 and 3.66, propositions 3.51, 3.58
and 3.60, and corollaries 3.44, 3.45 and 3.48, with the finite S3 certificates used only as explicit witnesses to the Artin-substitution obstruction.
Consequently the introduction theorem environments are assembly statements: no additional hypothesis, boundary value, branch convention, scalar
normalisation, or appendix input is hidden in their restatement. In particular, the authoritative proof obligations are the body statements listed
above, and every output in Main Theorems I–III factors through the finitedata chain of Proposition 3.31.
For (i), Proposition 3.1 is proved before any Perron-boundary assumption is imposed. Its proof cites precisely Lemma 3.6, theorems 3.7, 3.20
and 3.23, and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18. These statements establish, in order, the fixed-point trace identity, the finite determinant linearisation of
Lϕ , the Adams–Möbius identity for Πϕ , the irreducible determinant-germ
formula, the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient formula, and class-count recovery by finite character orthogonality. This is exactly the content of the
first introduction theorem.
For (ii), imposing λ > 1 and η < λ gives the non-trivial Perron-point values of the primitive channels by Theorem 3.33. The same theorem proves the
displayed class-Mertens asymptotic and the formula (39); Proposition 3.70
and theorem 3.72 then identify the normalised deviation profile and its Hermitian Fourier–Parseval transform. These are exactly the additional assertions made in the second introduction theorem, and their only new analytic
hypothesis is the strict finite-dimensional twisted gap already stated there.
For (iii), Theorem 3.39 proves the Frobenius-class Euler-product asymptotic with the fixed-label transform Fϱ (λ−1 ) and the product constant KC .
The determinant evaluation and finite certificate for those fixed-label Perron values are Propositions 3.58 and 3.60; substituting them into the class
product gives Theorem 3.43 and corollary 3.45. The comparison, stability, scalar-baseline readout, and obstruction clauses are precisely Proposition 3.51, corollaries 3.44 and 3.48, and theorem 3.66. The cited S3 statements supply concrete finite witnesses to that last obstruction and do not
add hypotheses to the product theorem. Finally, Proposition 3.31 proves
that these three tiers are connected by one finite-data dependency chain,
so the introduction restatements introduce no independent theorem beyond
the assembled body chain.
□

presented as a new Tauberian input: under the explicit finite-dimensional
spectral gap η < λ, it records the elementary Perron–Frobenius and Peter–
Weyl version needed here, with constants normalized for the later Adams–
Möbius determinant calculation. Its output is the additive vector (MC )C , or
equivalently the non-trivial Perron values (Πϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , which is the input
for the product-tail reduction in the next block.

Lemma 3.36 (Exponentiating the class product logarithm). Let a ∈ R,
L ∈ R, and let RN = O(N −1 ) be a real sequence. If a positive sequence QN
satisfies
log QN = −a log N + L + RN ,
then

QN = eL N −a 1 + O(N −1 ) .
If the implied constant in RN = O(N −1 ) is uniform over a finite set of
indices, then the resulting multiplicative error is uniform over that set.
Proof. Choose B > 0 with |RN | ≤ B/N for all large N . Then
QN = eL N −a eRN .
For all large N , |RN | ≤ 1, and the elementary estimate |ex − 1| ≤ e|x| for
|x| ≤ 1 gives eRN = 1 + O(N −1 ). The same bound with the maximum of
the finitely many constants gives the uniform assertion.

expansion of the right-hand side the scalar Perron logarithm is not evaluated: the term (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) has zero coefficient because ϕ ⊥ 1, and
it is deleted before the boundary value z = λ−1 is taken. All remaining

interior values with rm ≥ 2. Consequently the non-trivial term in the classproduct constant is a fixed-label Euler transform, and it may be replaced by
the periodic Artin logarithm only in the channels satisfying the coefficient

records this last sentence inside the chosen branch-compatible chart: after the
scalar coordinate is fixed, every bad replacement of the determinant boundary data is exactly a non-zero vector in the (q − 1)-dimensional zero-mean
class-function coordinate space, where q is the number of conjugacy classes
of G, and no scalar, exponent-level, or notation-level record can hide it there.
Proof. The displayed formulas are the definitions of the primitive channel
and fixed-label Euler transform together with Lemma 3.8, which gives the
Artin logarithm formula. Since ϕ is orthogonal to the trivial character and
η < λ, Theorem 3.11 gives absolute convergence of Fϕ (λ−1 ) and the determinant expression (50). In the expanded determinant expression the
coefficient of the single scalar boundary term is ⟨ϕ, 1⟩G = 0; hence that singular logarithm is omitted before the Abel limit is formed. The remaining
boundary terms have non-trivial twist and are regular by the strict twisted
gap, while every term with rm ≥ 2 lies strictly inside the Perron disc. This
is exactly the branch-compatible continuation used here. Finally, Proposition 3.12 proves that equality between Fϕ and Lϕ is equivalent to (23). Main
Theorem III uses precisely the values Fϱ (λ−1 ) in its non-trivial Fourier coordinates, so the stated product-constant roles follow. The final roadmap
sentence is the specialisation of Corollary 3.47 to the boundary-error vector Bϱ = Lϱ (λ−1 ), or more generally to any branch-compatible substitute
(Bϱ )ϱ̸=1 .

Before stating the product theorem, we separate the classical and finitelabel inputs. The exponent |C|/|G|, Chebotarev equidistribution, and the
existence scale of Frobenius-class Mertens products belong to the dynamical orbit-counting lineage of Parry–Pollicott, Noorani–Parry, Sharp, and
Mesliza–Noorani [31, 27, 41, 24]. The new step below is the finite determinant recovery of the product constants after this leading term and the scalar
normalisation C(A) have been separated: their non-trivial coordinates are
the fixed-label Euler transform values Fϱ (λ−1 ), not the periodic Artin logarithms Lϱ (λ−1 ).
3.5.3. Main Theorem III: fixed-label Euler-product constants. The notation
in the theorem is the fixed-label notation of Section 3.2. Here KC is the
positive real product constant, C(A) is the scalar finite-part normalisation,

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

55

χ∗ϱ (C) = χϱ (C) is the Hermitian class coefficient, and the non-trivial coordinates are Fϱ (λ−1 ). The compact proof spine is the finite map from the
classical product layer to the primitive product tail, then to the fixed-label
coordinates, and finally to twisted determinants; the arrows are made precise
by Lemmas 3.35 and 3.53 and propositions 3.41, 3.51 and 3.58:
classical product layer −→ primitive product tail
primitive product tail −→ fixed-label coordinates
fixed-label coordinates −→ twisted determinants.
Thus determinant recovery of these same coordinates is a later step in the
same theorem chain, not an additional definition of the class product.

Proposition 3.40 (Dependency map for Main Theorem III). Under the
hypotheses of Theorem 3.39, the proof of the product theorem factors through
the following finite list of interfaces, and no other analytic or certification
input is used:
(i) the classical Frobenius-class product layer supplies the existence of a
product constant and the exponent |C|/|G|;
(ii) Lemma 3.35 converts the logarithmic class-Mertens estimate and the
absolutely convergent r ≥ 2 product tail into (53);
(iii) Lemmas 3.53 and 3.54 convert the primitive-tail constant into the
fixed-label character expression (54);
(iv) Theorem 3.11 and proposition 3.58 identify each non-trivial coordinate
Fϱ (λ−1 ) with its Adams–Möbius determinant boundary value, with the
scalar Perron triple deleted before evaluation;
(v) Propositions 3.41 and 3.51 record the reversible fixed-coordinate form
and the separation between the inherited classical product theorem and
the new Adams-corrected determinant constant.
Consequently the Main Theorem III product constants are determined by the
scalar normalisation C(A), the finite character table and power maps of
G, and the finitely many twisted determinant polynomials det(I − zAπ ); replacing the fixed-label vector by any other branch-compatible vector changes
the class constants exactly by the Peter–Weyl error measured in Proposition 3.51.
Proof. Item (i) is the classical layer explicitly stated in Theorem 3.39. For
(ii), the logarithmic calculation in the proof of that theorem is precisely
Lemma 3.35 followed by Lemma 3.36, which gives the positive branch
formula (53). Item (iii) is Lemma 3.53; the displayed class-indicator
Fourier expansion in the theorem proof is the normalisation isolated in
Lemma 3.54. Item (iv) is exactly the boundary assertion of Theorem 3.11
together with the determinant formula of Proposition 3.58. Item (v) is the
closed-coordinate equivalence and comparison theorem cited there.
The final determination statement follows by composing these five arrows:
the scalar channel contributes only C(A), the non-trivial channels are the
determinant values of (iv), and the finite Fourier transform in (iii) uses
only the finite character table and class-power data. The last sentence is
Equation (80), applied to the difference between the true fixed-label vector
and a proposed branch-compatible substitute.
□

The leading product exponent and the existence of the positive constants
are exactly Theorem 3.39; the historical calibration is the one stated in
Proposition 3.51. The character formula (54) expresses (KC )C in terms of
C(A) and the non-trivial values Fϱ (λ−1 ). Replacing those values by Dϱ
using (58), and then taking logarithms in the positive real branch fixed by
Theorem 3.39, gives the first identity in (61). Averaging this identity over
conjugacy classes with weights |C|/|G| gives the second identity in (60),
because every non-trivial irreducible character is orthogonal to the trivial
character. Multiplying the definition of HK by |C|/|G| and summing over
classes then gives the product constraint in (60). The inverse Fourier identity
in (61) and the Parseval identity (63) are the standard Hermitian character
orthogonality relations for the zero-mean class-function space.
Solving the definition (59) for KC and substituting the first identity in
(61) gives (62). This proves that the product vector is determined by the
listed scalar normalisation, character table, Adams tensors, branch tags, and
twisted determinant polynomials. Conversely, if the positive constants and
C(A) are known, then (59) constructs HK , and the inverse Hermitian Fourier
formula in (61) recovers each Dπ . The final equal-data assertion follows by
applying the same displayed formulae to the two systems. No step uses any
presentation, cover, fold, gauge, or cocycle-conjugacy invariant beyond these
fixed coordinates, which proves the asserted scope of the rigidity statement.
□

3.5.5. Local equivalence and observation of product coordinates. The next
consequences describe the product-coordinate vector recovered in Main Theorem III. They identify the finite Fourier coordinates of the Adams-corrected
positive class constants and record the residual fibre inside that already recovered scalar-normalised chart. These are subordinate product-coordinate
consequences, not inverse spectral theorems or classification statements for
extensions or cocycles.
Proposition 3.46 (Fixed-coordinate equivalence after scalar normalisation). Under the hypotheses and notation of Theorem 3.43, consider two finite determinant-coordinate data sets with the same finite group G, the same
character table and Adams tensors, the same scalar normalisation C(A), and
the same normalized Abel/ordinary branch convention of Proposition 3.58.
Thus equality of a coordinate always means equality of the corresponding
branch-compatible logarithmic determinant value, not merely equality of an
exponentiated determinant. If their non-trivial fixed-label Perron vectors
agree,
Fϱ (λ−1 ) = Fϱ′ (λ−1 )
(ϱ ̸= 1),

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

65

then they give the same Frobenius-class product constants KC = KC′ for
every conjugacy class C. Conversely, if the constants agree for every C and
the same scalar normalisation C(A) is used, then the non-trivial fixed-label
Perron vectors agree. Thus the recoverable invariant at Main Theorem III
is exactly the fixed branch-normalised determinant coordinate vector modulo
this finite Fourier equivalence, not a presentation-, cover-, gauge-, branchfree, or fold-invariant classification of the underlying cocycle.
Proof. With C(A) fixed, equality of the fixed-label Perron vectors makes
the right-hand side of (61) identical for every class. Substitution in the
exponential formula for KC in Theorem 3.43 therefore gives KC = KC′ for
all C. Conversely, equality of the positive constants and equality of C(A)
imply equality of the normalised logarithmic class functions HK defined in
(59). Applying the inverse character formula in (61) gives

and the fixed scalar/branch normalisation. In particular, the implication is
false as a branch-free statement: changing logarithmic lifts can change the
Perron vector even when exponentiated determinant values are unchanged,
as in the standard fixed-matrix ambiguity of choosing logarithmic branches.
Thus no conclusion about alternative presentations, gauges, folds, branches,
or extensions is introduced.

Q

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

67

real logarithm in each coordinate, gives (|C|/|G|)(H(C) − H ′ (C)) = 0 for

Now let R : HG → W have the stated determining property. If V ∈ ker R,
then R(V ) = R(0), so the determining property gives Kα (V ) = Kα (0). By
injectivity of Kα , V = 0. Thus R is injective, and its rank is at least
dim HG = q − 1, proving (74).
Conversely, if a fixed-coordinate linear record is not injective, choose a
non-zero V ∈ ker R. For every H ∈ HG and every t ∈ R, linearity gives
R(H + tV ) = R(H). If s ̸= t, then H + sV ̸= H + tV ; by the injectivity
of Kα , already proved above, Kα (H + sV ) ̸= Kα (H + tV ). Thus every nonzero kernel vector produces the advertised one-parameter ambiguity family
of indistinguishable fixed-coordinate records with distinct positive product
constants. Taking, for instance, H = 0, s = 0, and t = 1 gives the stated bad
pair. This shows that the rank lower bound is exactly the obstruction to determining all scalar-normalised product constants in the fixed determinantcoordinate chart.
The finite character orthogonality relations identify the non-trivial irreducible characters with an orthonormal basis of the zero-mean class-function
space, with the standard conjugation symmetry when the original class function is real. Therefore the Peter–Weyl record (75) is an isometric injection
from HG onto a real coordinate space of dimension q − 1, so it attains the
lower bound.
For the dynamical constants, Theorem 3.43 identifies the coordinate tuple
of HK with (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , and Proposition 3.58 evaluates those coordinates
from the finite twisted determinant family. This gives the claimed ranksharp fixed-coordinate interpretation of the determinant data. Finally, EB
is the zero-mean class function whose non-trivial Fourier coordinates are
Fϱ (λ−1 ) − Bϱ . Applying Parseval in the same zero-mean Peter–Weyl space
proves (76). If the right-hand side is non-zero, some classwise error is nonzero, so the substitute boundary cannot give all scalar-normalised product
constants; scalar data live in the trivial one-dimensional summand and have
already been fixed by α.
□

3.5.6. Finite determinant closure. We now close the determinant-toconstant chain: the Adams-corrected fixed-label coordinates are computable from the finite twisted determinant family with the stated branch
convention.
Proposition 3.49 (Finite determinant closure of the main constants). Assume λ > 1 and the twisted spectral gap η < λ of Theorem 3.33. Encode the
finite group by its multiplication table and let the finite determinant datum
be

D(A, τ ; G) := G, {C ⊂ G conjugacy class}, (C, m) 7→ C m ,


C(A), {det(I − zAπ ) : π ∈ Irr(G)} .
Together with the Abel-path branch convention fixed separately in Theorem 3.33 and proposition 3.58, this finite datum determines the following
three nested output tiers. Each tier is asserted only under the hypotheses
and branch conventions of the theorem result cited for that tier.
(i) Algebraic determinant recovery: every primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient πn,ϱ (A, τ ) and hence every primitive class count pn,C ;
(ii) Perron-boundary recovery under the twisted gap: every non-trivial
Perron-point primitive value Πϱ (λ−1 ) and every class-Mertens constant MC (A, τ ; G);
(iii) Fixed-label product recovery: every fixed-label value Fϱ (λ−1 ) and every Frobenius-class Euler-product constant KC .
The recovery in item (i) is purely algebraic: coefficient extraction from the
finitely many determinant polynomials, finite character-table linear algebra,
Adams coefficients, Möbius inversion, and finite Fourier inversion. The

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

69

recovery in items (ii) and (iii) is the branch-compatible Perron-boundary
evaluation of those same finite determinant polynomials under η < λ, not
a claim that the boundary constants are finite algebraic expressions in the
polynomial coefficients alone. The singular scalar determinant logarithm
is never evaluated; the scalar normalisation uses the positive Perron constant C(A). Conversely, after the scalar normalisation C(A), the productconstant vector (KC )C determines exactly the non-trivial fixed-label Perron
vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 by the inverse Fourier formula in (78). Hence two extensions with identical data D(A, τ ; G), the same branch choices, and the
same scalar normalisation C(A) have identical outputs in Main Theorems I–
III. This proposition is only the closure of the assembled chain: the raw
determinant recovery is Corollary 3.18 and theorem 3.23, the Perron-point
convergence and additive constants are Theorems 3.33 and 3.72, and the
product-level fixed-label constants are Theorems 3.39 and 3.43 and proposition 3.58.
Proof. For tier (i), the finite group component of D(A, τ ; G) gives the complete character table, the irreducible characters, conjugacy classes, and the
Adams class maps C 7→ C m . The determinant polynomials
determine all
P
traces Tr(Anπ ) by the finite identity log det(I − zAπ )−1 = n≥1 Tr(Anπ )z n /n
on its formal power-series side. Lemma 3.6 identifies these traces with the
periodic twisted fixed-point sums. Finite character orthogonality then gives
Theorem 3.7, and the Adams–Möbius formula in Corollary 3.10 converts
the periodic class-function coefficients to primitive coefficients. Therefore
Corollaries 3.18 and 3.21 and theorem 3.23 recover all primitive Peter–Weyl
coefficients and primitive class counts from D(A, τ ; G).
For tier (ii), under η < λ, the non-trivial determinant series are regular at the Perron point along the branch specified in Theorem 3.33. That
theorem, together with Proposition 3.70 and theorem 3.72, evaluates the
non-trivial primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) and hence the class-Mertens constants.
For tier (iii), the fixed-label Euler-transform theorem Theorem 3.39 and the
finite determinant formula Proposition 3.58 evaluate Fϱ (λ−1 ) for every nontrivial ϱ; substituting these values into Theorem 3.43 gives the Frobeniusclass product constants KC . The scalar singular determinant is not evaluated in these steps: the scalar contribution is the supplied Perron constant C(A), exactly as in the displayed product formulas of Theorems 3.39
and 3.43. Thus the proof uses exactly the branch conventions and convergence hypotheses stated in the corresponding theorem of each tier.
Finally, the inverse formula (78) is a finite Fourier inversion on the class
algebra after the scalar baseline has been fixed. It shows that the vector
(KC )C determines precisely the non-trivial vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , and the direct product formula gives the converse dependence. Every operation used
above is therefore either finite coefficient extraction, finite character-table
algebra, Adams–Möbius inversion, or the already proved branch-compatible
Perron-point evaluation under η < λ. The proposition adds no new leading
asymptotic claim beyond the cited results: the Frobenius-density exponent
in the product theorem is classical, while the new finite-data content used
here is the Adams–Möbius fixed-label determinant recovery of the constants.

70

HAOBO MA

Identical finite collections with identical branch choices and scalar normalisation pass through the same sequence of operations, so they have identical
outputs in the three main theorems.
□
Remark 3.50 (Order of the finite recovery). Under the hypotheses of Proposition 3.49, the finite-data recovery factors through the following ordered
chain:
(77)

D(A, τ ; G) −→ OI −→ OII −→ OIII ,

where OI consists of the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficients and primitive class
counts, OII consists of the non-trivial Perron-point primitive values and
class-Mertens constants, and OIII consists of the fixed-label Perron values
and Frobenius-class product constants. The first arrow uses only coefficient
extraction, Adams coefficients, and finite Fourier inversion. The second arrow is the Abel-branch Perron-boundary evaluation supplied by the twisted
spectral gap. The third arrow is the fixed-label Euler-transform and classproduct formula of Theorem 3.39 and Theorem 3.43. Thus Proposition 3.49
and corollary 3.45 are finite recoveries of Main Theorems I–III, while Theorem 3.43 is the branch-compatible fixed-coordinate rigidity theorem for the
Adams-corrected product vector after scalar normalisation. In particular,
equality of two finite determinant data implies equality of all three ordered
outputs, and after C(A) is fixed equality of the product-constant vectors is
equivalent to equality of the non-trivial fixed-label Perron vectors.
The factor N −|C|/|G| and the existence of a Frobenius-class Mertens product asymptotic are the classical part of (52); compare the Frobenius-class
product theorem of Mesliza–Noorani [24], Sharp’s dynamical Mertens theorem [41], and the Chebotarev orbit-distribution antecedents [31, 27]. Nearby
finite-data and skew-product works such as [9, 28] address different rigidity
or extension questions. The finite-state primitive-label contribution needed
here is the following named comparison theorem: it is the Adams correction
to the Frobenius-class Mertens product, not merely a restatement of the
classical density theorem.
Proposition 3.51 (Adams correction to Frobenius-class Mertens products). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.39, the following assertions hold
after the classical asymptotic form and the scalar base constant have been
separated. For the topologically mixing SFT defined by A, with finite group
extension determined by τ , primitive closed orbits weighted by λ−|γ| , and
Frobenius class restricted to a conjugacy class C ⊂ G, the product existence
and the leading exponent |C|/|G| are exactly the classical Frobenius-class
product layer supplied by Mesliza–Noorani’s theorem on Mertens products
in Frobenius classes [24], in the Parry–Pollicott/Noorani–Parry Chebotarev
symbolic-extension setting and with Sharp’s symbolic-dynamical Mertens
normalisation [31, 27, 41]. In this notation their layer is the assertion that
PC (N )N |C|/|G| has a positive finite limit, after the same Perron normalisation by λ; the sharper finite-dimensional gap hypotheses of Theorem 3.33
give the explicit 1 + O(N −1 ) remainder used here. This proposition does not
assert the Frobenius-class Mertens asymptotic itself as new. The finite-state
assertion of this proposition is the remaining fixed-coordinate statement:

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

values are recovered from finite twisted determinants by Adams–Möbius
inversion, and every proposed replacement is measured by the zero-mean
Peter–Weyl error vector below. Thus the new datum is the Adams-corrected
fixed-label determinant constant and its obstruction to Artin substitution,
not the Frobenius-class Mertens asymptotic itself. The substitution test is
downstream and is not a hypothesis for Main Theorems I–III.

Proof. The ordered assertions below separate the inherited and finite-label
parts of the product theorem. Mesliza–Noorani’s Frobenius-class Mertens
product theorem supplies, for the same primitive-orbit product and Perron
normalisation, the existence of the Frobenius-class product constant and
the exponent |C|/|G|. The symbolic Chebotarev setting is the one of Parry–
Pollicott and Noorani–Parry, and the scalar Mertens normalisation is the
Sharp normalisation used throughout the paper. The stronger explicit error term in Theorem 3.39 is obtained internally from Theorem 3.33 and
Lemma 3.35; no step in the proof reclaims the classical existence/exponent
layer. What remains to prove is that, after the scalar baseline is removed,
the branch-compatible class constants are equivalent to the non-trivial fixedlabel Perron vector, that this vector is the Adams–Möbius determinant vector, and that every substitute vector has exactly the Peter–Weyl error stated
in (C)–(D). Since (C) and (D) are obtained only after subtracting a proposed
replacement vector from the fixed-label vector, they are downstream tests
rather than inputs to the proof of Theorem 3.39.

□
Thus Main Theorems I–III are finite determinant-to-primitive, Perronpoint, and Adams-corrected product-constant theorems. The shadow
results Theorems 5.4, 5.8 and 5.10, the eta-gap tests Theorem 4.1, corollary 4.2, and proposition 4.6, and the S3 rational checks Theorem 5.14,
propositions 5.15 and A.4, and corollary 5.16 serve this determinant and
product-coordinate picture. They are not asserted as new geometric
periodic-orbit invariants, Ruelle-resonance theorems, finite-data rigidity
theorems, or operator-algebraic classifications.
Remark 3.52 (Comparison with the literature for the main theorems). The
comparison references below locate the inherited symbolic-dynamical layers
of the argument, while the finite-label determinant interfaces are the contributions used in the main theorem chain.
(i) Main Theorem I and Corollaries 3.18 and 3.21 and theorem 3.23
use Parry–Pollicott and Noorani–Parry only for the classical finite
symbolic-extension setting; the asserted contribution is the finite

74

HAOBO MA

Peter–Weyl determinant inversion with Adams–Möbius correction for
primitive repetitions.
(ii) Main Theorem II and Theorems 3.33 and 3.72 and proposition 3.70
use Sharp’s Mertens scale and the Chebotarev/Mertens lineage only
for the leading density, exponent, and strict-gap context; the asserted
contribution is the class-indexed constant obtained from non-trivial
primitive-channel Perron values.
(iii) Main Theorem III and Theorems 3.39 and 3.66 and proposition 3.58
use the Frobenius-class product comparison only for product-existence
and normalisation context;Pthe asserted contribution is the fixed-label
Euler transform Fϱ (z) = r≥1 Πϱ (z r )/r, its determinant evaluation
at z = λ−1 , and the obstruction to substituting the ordinary periodic
Artin logarithm Lϱ (λ−1 ) for Fϱ (λ−1 ).
(iv) The eta-gap, shadow, and S3 rational-check results support the same
determinant/product-coordinate picture. The recent comparison papers on geometric periodic-orbit invariants, Ruelle resonances, finitedata rigidity, and non-commutative skew products are not hypotheses
and are not claimed as consequences.

3.5.8. Determinant evaluation of fixed-label Perron values. This unit is the
computational part of Main Theorem III. It evaluates the fixed-label numbers Fϱ (λ−1 ) from the same finite twisted determinant data used in Main
Theorem I. The local branch convention is fixed before the formula is written: all determinant logarithms are continued along the Abel path from the
open disc, the scalar Perron determinant is not present in the non-trivial
boundary coordinate, and the only omitted summand is the singular triple
(r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1). This is not a replacement of Fϱ by the periodic Artin logarithm; it is the Adams-corrected determinant expansion of the fixed-label
transform.
Lemma 3.57 (Singular-triple deletion and branch convention). Assume the
hypotheses of Theorem 3.33, and fix a non-trivial irreducible representation
ϱ ∈ Irr(G). For 0 < t < 1, the Abel-regularized fixed-label determinant

the boundary limit is taken. All remaining rm = 1 terms are non-trivial
twisted branches regular at z = λ−1 , and all rm ≥ 2 terms, including scalar

disc. The deleted-series convention therefore has a unique Abel limit, and
the convergence to that limit is justified by an absolute majorant independent
of t ∈ (0, 1).
