The branch convention is local to this proposition. For each displayed determinant, start with the analytic germ Log det(I − zAπ )−1 = 0 at z = 0. If
rm = 1 and π ̸= 1, its value is the Abel-path continuation to z = λ−1 along

spectral gap. If rm ≥ 2, the value is the ordinary value of the same normalized germ at z = λ−rm , where ρ(λ−rm Aπ ) < 1. If ϱ ̸= 1, an Adams power
ψ m χϱ may contain the trivial character for m ≥ 2. The corresponding scalar

Proof. Apply Lemma 3.57 to the Abel identity (87). That lemma makes
the local deletion before the boundary limit explicit: the only scalar Perron
logarithm is (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1), its coefficient is zero by non-trivial-channel
orthogonality, and every scalar term with rm ≥ 2 lies strictly inside the
Perron disc. The same lemma supplies the branch convention for the nontrivial rm = 1 terms and the absolute majorant for the rm ≥ 2 double tail.
Dominated convergence as t ↑ 1, together with the existence of the nontrivial Perron value in Theorem 3.11, proves (88) with exactly the displayed
exclusion and branch choices.
□
The determinant form of Main Theorem III uses one fixed normalization.
The sign of KC is the outer minus sign in (54), equivalently the positive

80

HAOBO MA

exponential of the negative logarithmic constant in (81). Class indicators
use the coefficient (|C|/|G|)χ∗ϱ (C), with inverse Fourier recovery performed
by summing against χσ (C). In Fϱ (λ−1 ), the single scalar boundary triple
(r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) is omitted before evaluation because its coefficient is
zero for ϱ ̸= 1. Non-trivial rm = 1 determinants use the Abel boundary
value of the normalized germ at the origin; all rm ≥ 2 determinants use the
ordinary interior-disc value. These conventions are exactly those proved in
Lemmas 3.54, 3.56 and 3.57 and proposition 3.58, and the absolute majorant
in Lemma 3.57 justifies the displayed order of summation and Abel limiting.
Corollary 3.59 (Convention compatibility for Main Theorem III). In Main
Theorem III, the following product-constant conventions are forced and mutually compatible.
(i) The class-indicator coefficient of a non-trivial irreducible character is
(|C|/|G|)χ∗ϱ (C), where χ∗ϱ (C) = χϱ (C).
(ii) The product constant uses the single outer minus sign in (54), with
scalar base γ + log C(A).
(iii) The determinant formula for Fϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1, deletes only the scalar
Perron triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) before evaluation; non-trivial boundary terms use the Abel branch from the germ at the origin and all
rm ≥ 2 terms use ordinary interior-disc values.
(iv) With these choices, the character formula for every KC is the positive real Euler-product constant of Theorem 3.39, and the scalarnormalised product vector is Fourier-equivalent to the non-trivial fixedlabel Perron vector.
Proof. Item (i) is exactly the Hermitian class-indicator normalisation of
Lemma 3.54. Item (ii) is Lemma 3.56. Item (iii) combines the scalar-triple
deletion rule of Lemma 3.57 with the determinant evaluation and branch
statement in Proposition 3.58. The real positive branch in item (iv) is
Lemma 3.55, while the Fourier equivalence is Proposition 3.51 and theorem 3.43. Hence all convention choices used by Main Theorem III are consequences of previously proved finite-state statements and cannot be varied
independently.
□

record with the same determinant polynomials, class-power maps, character

the same absolutely convergent determinant series (88). Each certified disc
therefore contains the unique value Fϱ (λ−1 ); in particular two such discs

intersect in that value, regardless of harmless choices in interval subdivision
or in the order of finite summation.
It remains only to justify the stated certificate format. For fixed M , the
set of triples (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M is finite, all Adams coefficients in it are
computed by finite character-table sums, and each determinant logarithm
is evaluated on the branch fixed in item (c). Replacing each such logarithm
by an interval of radius ϵ0 changes the weighted finite sum by at most the
triangle-inequality bound in (95). Choosing ϵ0 so that this finite bound
is at most the remaining budget after the rational tail radius in (90), and
choosing M by that rational inequality, gives a rigorous output interval of
radius at most ϵ. Thus the input, branch convention, finite output, and
error bound are all finite and explicit.

Appendix-level reproducibility records. The local rational reproducibility
record and five-field S3 certificate format are placed in Propositions C.1,
C.2 and C.4 and corollary C.5. They are appendix-level certificate formats
for the finite determinant computability theorem Proposition 3.60; the main
proof path uses only the determinant formula, the finite truncation bound,
and finite character-table Fourier inversion.

the rational records used in this paper.

where Iϱ is the certified finite determinant sum. Applying the accepted

gives certified rational enclosures for the logarithms of all Frobenius-class
product constants KC . Conversely, every finite record accepted by the appendix record format in Propositions C.1 and C.4 and corollary C.5 is exactly a

Proof. The first three finite checks are precisely the finite input, coefficient,
deletion, and branch requirements in Proposition 3.60. The only scalar
boundary singularity is the triple (1, 1, 1), and it is deleted before any logarithm is evaluated; for ϱ ̸= 1 the coefficient of this deleted term is zero
by character orthogonality. The strict twisted gap gives the Abel continuation of the remaining rm = 1 non-trivial blocks, and the inequality
ρ(λ−rm Aπ ) ≤ λ1−rm < 1 gives the ordinary logarithmic germ for rm ≥ 2.
The fourth finite check is the effective tail and roundoff estimate proved
in Proposition 3.60. Therefore the accepted finite sum Iϱ , enlarged by
RM,ϱ + Rnum,ϱ , contains the unique Perron value Fϱ (λ−1 ). Since the nontrivial irreducible set is finite, applying this argument to every ϱ ̸= 1 gives
a certified enclosure for the whole fixed-label vector. The passage from this
vector to (log KC )C in Corollary 3.62 is finite character-table linear algebra together with the fixed scalar normalisation C(A), so ordinary interval
arithmetic propagates the certified radii to all product constants without
any primitive-orbit enumeration or limiting orbit count.
Finally, Propositions C.1 and C.4 and corollary C.5 record exactly the
same model, window, Adams, branch, and bound fields in appendix notation.
Their converses state that every accepted appendix finite record determines
these fields, and the proof above shows that these fields determine the same
enclosure. This proves both the product-level verification statement and its
equivalence with the appendix finite records.

3.5.9. Product-vector rigidity after scalar normalisation. After the
determinant-only evaluation of Fϱ (λ−1 ), the remaining statements in
this unit concern the finite class vector (KC )C . They are rigidity and
observation results for the already constructed product constants: they
record the scalar hyperplane constraint, Fourier inversion on conjugacy
classes, and the finite coordinate fibre of the whole positive product vector
inside the branch-compatible determinant chart. They do not assert
recovery of the cocycle or any branch-free inverse spectral datum.

formulas already proved in Theorems 3.33 and 3.39. In the refined summatory identity (109) the auxiliary contribution is the single compensated tail

already absorbed in the displayed o(1) term. Therefore the limiting positive
product constant is still the value in (111).

lower-order tail is chosen. The inverse Fourier recovery of Theorem 3.43 then
recovers the same fixed-label vector from the normalized product constants.
Thus any two auxiliary refinements satisfying the tail condition have identical main Mertens constants, product constants, and recovered fixed-label
Perron values.
□

Remark 3.68 (Obstruction refinements). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.39, the core Main Theorem III data consist of the Frobenius-class
product asymptotic, the fixed-label constants KC , the finite determinant
evaluation of Fϱ (λ−1 ), and the Adams–Möbius obstruction to the naive
Artin substitution. The fixed-coordinate fibre corollary Corollary 3.47 and
the Parseval bound of Corollary 3.67 are determined functorially by those
data together with the already defined periodic Artin logarithms: if two
finite-state extensions have the same scalar baseline γ + log C(A), the same
non-trivial fixed-label boundary values Fϱ (λ−1 ), and the same periodic
Artin logarithms Lϱ (λ−1 ), then they have the same scalar-normalised fixedcoordinate product vector in the branch-compatible determinant chart, the
same class constants, and the same Artin-substitution obstruction bound.
Conversely, equality of the scalar-normalised fixed-coordinate product
vector in that fixed chart forces equality of all non-trivial fixed-label
boundary values. Hence the fixed-coordinate fibre corollary and the
Parseval obstruction bound are downstream corollary-level refinements of
Main Theorem III, not independent main theorems and not additional
hypotheses for the product asymptotic.
Main Theorem III, in the form of Theorem 3.39, gives the constants

Remark 3.71 (On the twisted spectral-gap hypothesis). The condition
η = max ρ(Aϱ ) < λ
ϱ̸=1

does not enter the determinant identities themselves: the formulas of Theorem 3.7 and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18 are valid throughout the disc |z| < λ−1
without it. The role of the hypothesis is exactly to permit evaluation at the
Perron point z = λ−1 in the non-trivial channels and to convert the primitive Peter–Weyl series into the class-indexed constants MC (A, τ ; G). From
the dynamical point of view this is the representation-by-representation analogue of separating the Perron eigenvalue from the remaining spectrum.
The leading Chebotarev and Frobenius-class Mertens phenomena for finite homogeneous extensions belong to the existing orbit-counting lineage,
beginning here with Noorani–Parry [27] and Noorani’s Markov-shift extension criteria [26], and continuing through the Axiom A, Livšic, skew-product
reciprocity, and homology-class settings of [31, 30, 33, 34, 36, 18, 42, 35, 7].
The present paper uses the gap as a stated finite-dimensional hypothesis,
not as a new structural criterion for cocycles. In the finite-state setting
it is already checkable: Lemma 3.4 identifies it with the absence of none equivalently
trivial peripheral spectrum in the skew-product adjacency A,
e
with A having a simple strictly dominant Perron eigenvalue, and, when Ae is
e What remains outside the present arguirreducible, with primitivity of A.
ment is a structural criterion on τ that would imply this condition without
first testing the full skew-product matrix. The new step here is instead
the finite-state Adams–Möbius calculus at the level of primitive constants
and the explicit class-indexed determinant formula MC (A, τ ; G), not a new
leading-density theorem of the type considered in [20, 8].
3.6. Hermitian Fourier readout of class constants. The class-indexed
constants MC (A, τ ; G) form the readout interface between Main Theorems II and III. Main Theorem II first produces their deviations from the
trivial average as Perron-point primitive channels, using the Adams–Möbius
determinant formula and the strict twisted gap. The present subsection
then applies Hermitian character orthogonality to read those channels back
from the class constants. This is the final step in Main Theorem II and
the vector of Perron-point values that Main Theorem III inserts into the
fixed-label Euler product; it is not a cyclic one-layer sampling argument.

Together with Theorem 3.33 and Proposition 3.70, this proves Main Theorem II from the introduction. The determinant and Adams–Möbius work
has already produced the Perron-point primitive channels; the final step
above is the standard character-orthogonality recovery on class functions,
not the one-layer DFT calculation used later for the canonical cyclic-lift
comparison.
Remark 3.73 (recovery analogy stops at the orthogonality step). Theorem 3.72 has the same formal linear-algebra shape as the root-of-unity
calculations for fixed-matrix cyclic-lift reconstruction. In the separate
canonical cyclic-lift comparison case, ordinary Fourier inversion acts
on root-of-unity layers of the single matrix A. Here the full family of
conjugacy-class Mertens constants MC (A, τ ; G) recovers the non-trivial
twisted primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) by non-abelian character orthogonality.
The analogy is only at the final recovery step: the class-indexed constants
being inverted here have first been produced by the Adams–Möbius
determinant mechanism and the twisted gap, whereas those fixed-matrix
calculations are not part of the finite-group theorem chain proved in this
article.
4. Finite-state criteria for the twisted spectral gap
The strict inequality η = maxϱ̸=1 ρ(Aϱ ) < λ is the finite-state hypothesis
that permits the non-trivial Peter–Weyl channels in Main Theorem II, and
hence the fixed-label Euler coordinates in Main Theorem III, to be evaluated
at the Perron point; if G is trivial, this empty maximum is 0. This section
gives finite-state criteria for that hypothesis and describes the equality case
η = λ. The equality case is rigid in the precise sense needed here: a nontrivial channel has Perron spectral radius exactly when the cocycle admits
an equivariant line field. The resulting loop-character criteria are used as
finite checks for the Perron-point hypotheses in Main Theorems II–III.

Corollary 4.2 (Finite-state criterion for the strict twisted gap). This is
the operational test for the gap hypothesis. It contains no product-constant
formula; it only decides whether the finite extension has a non-trivial Perronboundary channel. Thus the boundary criterion below is used only to certify
the hypothesis of Main Theorems II–III before the Adams–Möbius determinant recovery is applied. Suppose G is non-trivial. Then the following are
equivalent.
(1) The Perron-point input used in the class-product theorem fails,
namely η = λ.
(2) There exist a non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ ∈ Irr(G), a
phase ω ∈ T, and unit vectors ξi ∈ Vϱ , one for each vertex, such
that
(127)

ϱ(τ (e))ξj = ωξi

for every admissible edge e : i → j.

100

HAOBO MA

Consequently, for this fixed finite-state cocycle, η < λ holds exactly when
the finite list of edge equations (127) has no solution in any non-trivial irreducible representation. In this case the hypotheses of Theorem 3.39 are
reduced to the verified absence of these boundary line fields.
Proof. By Proposition 3.5, every non-trivial channel satisfies ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ.
Since G is finite, there are only finitely many irreducible representations, so
the maximum defining η is achieved. Hence η = λ if and only if ρ(Aϱ ) = λ
for at least one non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G). Applying Theorem 4.1 to that representation gives exactly the edge equations (127), and the converse implication in the same theorem shows that any solution of those equations forces
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, hence η = λ. The final sentence is the contrapositive, together
with the role of the strict-gap hypothesis stated in Theorem 3.39.
□
Corollary 4.3 (Loop-character classification of boundary channels). Fix a
non-trivial irreducible unitary representation ϱ : G → U (Vϱ ). For a phase
ω ∈ T and a vertex i, let
Wi (ϱ, ω) :=

\

γ:i→i

where the intersection is over all closed admissible paths based at i. Then
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ if and only if there are a phase ω ∈ T and unit vectors ξi ∈
Wi (ϱ, ω), one for each vertex, such that
ϱ(τ (e))ξj = ωξi

(e : i → j).

For every such solution, the line Li := Cξi is a one-dimensional Γi (τ )subrepresentation of ϱ|Γi (τ ) , with character χi : Γi (τ ) → T determined by
χi (τ (γ)) = ω |γ|

(γ : i → i).

Moreover, if p : i → j is any admissible path of length |p|, then ω −|p| ϱ(τ (p))
gives a unitary similarity Lj → Li . Thus the Perron-boundary obstruction
is precisely a compatible system of one-dimensional loop characters and edge
similarities.
Proof. If ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, then Theorem 4.1 supplies ω and unit vectors ξi satisfying the displayed edge equations. Iterating those equations around a closed
path γ : i → i gives
ϱ(τ (γ))ξi = ω |γ| ξi ,
so ξi ∈ Wi (ϱ, ω) for every i. Conversely, if such unit vectors in the spaces
Wi (ϱ, ω) satisfy the edge equations, the converse half of Theorem 4.1 gives
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ.
It remains only to identify the character data. The closed-path identity
just displayed says that every generator τ (γ) of Γi (τ ) preserves Li and acts
on it by the scalar ω |γ| . Products and inverses of such generators therefore
also preserve Li , and the scalar by which an element of Γi (τ ) acts on the
one-dimensional space Li defines a well-defined group homomorphism χi :
Γi (τ ) → T. This homomorphism has the stated value on every closed-path
label. Finally, iterating the edge equations along a path p : i → j gives
ϱ(τ (p))ξj = ω |p| ξi ; hence ω −|p| ϱ(τ (p)) maps the unit line Lj unitarily onto
Li . This proves the announced classification.

Proposition 4.4 (Loop-subgroup obstruction to Perron-boundary channels). Suppose G is non-trivial, and fix a vertex i0 . Put H := Γi0 (τ ) and
H ′ := [H, H]. If the Perron-boundary obstruction η = λ occurs, then there
is a non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ ∈ Irr(G) such that
′

(Vϱ )H := {v ∈ Vϱ : ϱ(h)v = v for every h ∈ H ′ }
is non-zero. Equivalently, if for some vertex i0 every non-trivial irreducible
representation ϱ of G satisfies (Vϱ )[Γi0 (τ ),Γi0 (τ )] = 0, then η < λ. In that case
the strict twisted spectral-gap hypothesis required in Main Theorems II–III,
and hence in Theorems 3.33 and 3.39, is certified by this single loop-subgroup
test.
Proof. Assume first that η = λ. By Corollary 4.2, there are a non-trivial
irreducible representation ϱ, a phase ω ∈ T, and a boundary line field (ξi )i .
Applying Corollary 4.3 at the fixed vertex i0 , the line Li0 := Cξi0 is invariant
under H = Γi0 (τ ), and the action of H on that line is by a one-dimensional
character χ : H → T. Since T is abelian, χ is trivial on the commutator
subgroup H ′ = [H, H]. Hence every element of H ′ fixes the non-zero vector
′
ξi0 , so ξi0 ∈ (Vϱ )H and the displayed fixed-vector space is non-zero.
The equivalent obstruction statement is the contrapositive of the first
assertion, together with the finite-state boundary criterion Corollary 4.2:
if the fixed-vector space above vanishes for every non-trivial irreducible ϱ,
then the boundary case η = λ cannot occur. Since Proposition 3.5 gives
ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ in every non-trivial channel and the maximum defining η is finite,
exclusion of equality is exactly η < λ. The final sentence records the role of
this certified strict gap in the two Perron-point product theorems cited in
the statement.
□
Corollary 4.5 (Perfect loop groups force the eta gap). Suppose G is nontrivial. Assume that for some vertex i0 the loop-label subgroup Γi0 (τ ) is
perfect and that, for every non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ of G, the
restricted representation ϱ|Γi0 (τ ) has no non-zero fixed vector. Then η < λ.
In particular, if Γi0 (τ ) = G for some vertex i0 and G is perfect, then the
strict eta-gap hypothesis required in Main Theorems II–III holds.
Proof. Let H = Γi0 (τ ). Since H is perfect, [H, H] = H. The fixed-vector
hypothesis is therefore precisely the vanishing condition in Proposition 4.4,
and that proposition gives η < λ.
For the stated special case, let Γi0 (τ ) = G and assume that G is perfect.
If a non-trivial irreducible ϱ had a non-zero G-fixed vector, the span of that
vector would be a non-zero invariant subspace on which G acts trivially;
irreducibility would force ϱ itself to be the trivial representation. This is
impossible for ϱ ̸= 1. Thus the fixed-vector hypothesis in the first part is
automatic, and the strict gap follows.
□

of the spectra of the blocks Aϱ , each repeated dim ϱ times. The trivial block

block and makes it simple. Thus all remaining roots of χAe lie inside |t| < λ
precisely when every non-trivial block has spectral radius strictly smaller

The equivalence of (i) and (iii) is Corollary 4.2 when non-trivial irreducible
representations exist. If none exist, then G is trivial, η = 0 by convention,
and the finite line-field condition is vacuous, so both (i) and (iii) hold because
λ > 1. In the remaining case, Theorem 4.1 says that equality ρ(Aϱ ) = λ for a
fixed non-trivial irreducible representation is equivalent to the displayed linefield equations for that representation. Since G is finite, only finitely many
irreducible representations occur, and the maximum defining η is attained.
Therefore the strict gap fails exactly when one of these finite systems has a
solution.
The final assertion is a substitution of this certificate into the hypotheses
of Theorems 3.33 and 3.39. Those theorems require λ > 1 and η < λ for the
Perron-point evaluation of the non-trivial primitive Peter–Weyl channels;
the present proposition only identifies that hypothesis with finite matrix or
finite algebraic tests. The cited loop-subgroup obstruction proves a sufficient
condition for item (i), and the perfect-loop criterion is its immediate structural specialization. None of these boundary criteria supplies an additional
constant formula when the tests fail.

Proposition 4.7 (Perron-point separation argument for Main Theorems
II–III). Assume λ > 1. The strict twisted spectral gap η < λ is equivalent to
each of the following Perron-point analytic conditions.
(i) For every non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ, the branch
log det(I − zAϱ )−1 =

n
X Tr(An
ϱ )z
n≥1

n

used in Main Theorem I extends holomorphically to a neighbourhood
of the closed disc |z| ≤ λ−1 .
(ii) For every non-trivial class function ϕ with zero average on G, all
non-trivial Peter–Weyl determinant coordinates entering the Adams–
Möbius formula for Πϕ have branch-compatible values at z = λ−1 , and
hence the Abel-path Perron values used in Theorems 3.33 and 3.39 are
computed entirely inside the finite determinant data.
If the strict gap fails, then there are a non-trivial irreducible representation
ϱ, a phase ω ∈ T, and a boundary line field satisfying (125). Moreover
ωλ ∈ spec(Aϱ ), so the non-trivial determinant factor det(I − zAϱ )−1 has a
pole at z = (ωλ)−1 on the Perron circle. Thus the finite-state certificate of
Proposition 4.6 is a necessary and sufficient verification gate for the Perronpoint constants in Main Theorems II–III; it is not an automatic existence
theorem for arbitrary cocycles.
Proof. The equivalence between η < λ and item (i) is purely finite dimensional. If η < λ, then every eigenvalue α of every non-trivial block Aϱ
satisfies |α| ≤ ρ(Aϱ ) < λ. Hence 1/α, for α ̸= 0, lies strictly outside the
Q
closed disc |z| ≤ λ−1 . The determinant det(I − zAϱ ) = α (1 − zα) therefore has no zero on a neighbourhood of that closed disc, and the logarithm
branch normalized at 0 extends holomorphically there.
Conversely, if item (i) holds and some non-trivial Aϱ had ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, then
an eigenvalue α ∈ spec(Aϱ ) would satisfy |α| = λ. The determinant det(I −
zAϱ ) would vanish at z = α−1 , a point of the Perron circle, so the reciprocal
determinant and its normalized logarithm could not be holomorphic in any
neighbourhood of the closed Perron disc. This contradicts item (i). Since
Proposition 3.5 gives ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ for every ϱ, item (i) forces η < λ.
Item (ii) is exactly the same condition after finite character decomposition.
The zero-average class functions have no trivial character coordinate. Their
periodic logarithms are finite linear combinations of the non-trivial determinant logarithms by Theorem 3.7, and the primitive series are then obtained
from those finitely many logarithms by the Adams–Möbius formula of Corollary 3.10. Therefore the Perron values used by Theorems 3.33 and 3.39 are
available from the determinant data precisely when the non-trivial determinant branches in item (i) extend through the closed Perron disc. The
trivial channel is deliberately excluded here; it is singular at z = λ−1 and is
handled separately by the scalar finite-part normalisation.
Finally suppose the strict gap fails. Because only finitely many irreducibles occur and each non-trivial block has spectral radius at most λ,
there is a non-trivial ϱ with ρ(Aϱ ) = λ. Theorem 4.1 supplies a phase ω
and a boundary line field satisfying (125); the converse half of its proof constructs an eigenvector of the column realization with eigenvalue ωλ. The

104

HAOBO MA

row and column realizations have the same spectrum, so ωλ ∈ spec(Aϱ ).
Hence det(I − zAϱ ) = 0 at z = (ωλ)−1 , and the reciprocal determinant
has a boundary pole. This is precisely the obstruction detected by Proposition 4.6.
□
Corollary 4.8 (Line fields are the only Perron-boundary obstruction). Under the standing hypotheses with λ > 1, the results of this section export
exactly the following finite-state alternative to the main theorem chain:
(i) either no non-trivial boundary line field exists, equivalently η < λ, and
the Perron-point hypotheses of Theorems 3.33 and 3.39 are verified;
(ii) or a non-trivial boundary line field exists, equivalently η = λ, and some
non-trivial determinant factor has a pole on the Perron circle, so the
Perron-point determinant recovery required by the class-Mertens and
class-product constants is not supplied by this paper.
In particular, the loop-character and loop-subgroup statements in this section
are criterion-level consequences of the boundary alternative. They are not
structural sufficient conditions for η < λ except where the stated hypotheses
explicitly eliminate every possible boundary line field.
Proof. Corollary 4.2 proves that the absence of non-trivial boundary line
fields is equivalent to η < λ, while the existence of such a line field is equivalent to η = λ. The first alternative is exactly the strict-gap hypothesis
invoked in Theorems 3.33 and 3.39. For the second alternative, Proposition 4.7 shows that the same line field produces a non-trivial eigenvalue on
the Perron circle and hence a boundary pole in the corresponding determinant factor. Therefore the non-trivial Perron branches used by the main
product theorem are available precisely in the first alternative. The loopcharacter classifier and the loop-subgroup obstruction are proved by starting
from such a boundary line field and recording necessary consequences, or by
imposing explicit hypotheses that rule out all such fields. Thus no broader
strict-gap criterion is asserted.
□
Corollary 4.9 (Perron-point availability for the main constants). Assume
G is non-trivial. For the fixed finite edge cocycle of this paper, the etagap section exports to Main Theorems II–III exactly the following boundary
interface:
(i) The operational finite-state certificate of Proposition 4.6 succeeds if
and only if the strict-gap hypothesis η < λ is available and if and
only if all non-trivial determinant logarithms have the Perron-point
branches certified by Proposition 4.7.
(ii) On this successful branch, every non-trivial fixed-label value Fϱ (λ−1 ),
every class-Mertens constant MC (A, τ ; G), and every product constant
KC is the value already prescribed by the Adams–Möbius determinant
formulas, the fixed-label Euler transform, and class-function Fourier
inversion. Thus the eta-gap section contributes only an availability
flag; it contributes no independent numerical coordinate to the product
vector.
(iii) On the failed branch, a non-trivial boundary line field exists and a nontrivial determinant factor has a pole on the Perron circle. Hence the

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

105

branch-compatible Perron-point vector needed for Main Theorems II–
III is not supplied by the hypotheses of this paper, while Main Theorem I remains unchanged because it uses only open-disc determinant
identities.
Broader structural criteria forcing η < λ for general cocycle classes are
outside the scope of this article; only the explicit line-field exclusions proved
in this section are retained as finite-state checks.
Proof. Proposition 4.6 proves that passing the operational certificate is exactly η < λ. The equivalence with the existence of the required Perron-point
determinant branches is Proposition 4.7. This proves item (i).
Under these equivalent conditions, the role of the eta-gap certificate in
the article is the role stated in Theorems 3.33 and 3.39: it permits evaluation of the non-trivial primitive Peter–Weyl channels at z = λ−1 . The
values of those channels are then fixed by the finite determinant formulas
of Proposition 3.58 and corollary 3.42, inserted into the Fourier formulas of
Proposition 3.70 and theorem 3.72, and finally inserted into the fixed-label
Euler transform of Theorem 3.39 and proposition 3.38. Each cited formula
is a function of the finite twisted determinant data and the scalar normalisation, not of any additional line-field datum. Therefore the eta-gap section
has rank zero as a numerical source for the product constants: after success
it supplies only the permission to evaluate the already fixed determinant
branches. This proves item (ii).
If the certificate fails, then Proposition 4.6 and theorem 4.1 give a nontrivial boundary line field. By Proposition 4.7, the same line field produces a
pole of a non-trivial determinant factor on the Perron circle, so the simultaneous branch-compatible Perron-point vector required by Main Theorems II–
III is absent from the hypotheses of this paper. The open-disc determinant
identities of Main Theorem I do not evaluate at λ−1 , so they are unaffected.
This proves item (iii). The final scope sentence follows from Corollary 4.8
and proposition 4.10, which state that this section proves only the finite alternative and its explicit line-field exclusions, not a broad sufficient-condition
theory.
□
Proposition 4.10 (Fixed-cocycle scope of the eta-gap certificate). The
eta-gap results in this section give, for the finite edge cocycle fixed in the
paper, exactly the following finite equality test and Perron-point gate, and
no broader classification of gap-producing cocycles.
(i) If the finite-state certificate of Proposition 4.6 succeeds, then η < λ,
and the Perron-point evaluations used in Main Theorems II–III are
justified by Proposition 4.7.
(ii) If the certificate fails, then some non-trivial irreducible channel admits
the boundary line field of Theorem 4.1. In that case this article makes
no class-Mertens or class-product assertion for the failed cocycle beyond the open-disc determinant identities of Main Theorem I.
(iii) The only sufficient implications for the strict inequality proved here are
fixed-cocycle eliminations of the classified boundary line fields, such as
the loop-character obstruction and the perfect-loop-group consequence.

106

HAOBO MA

A structural theory forcing η < λ for general cocycle classes is not part
of this article.
Proof. By Proposition 4.6, success of the operational finite-state test is equivalent to the absence of a non-trivial Perron boundary channel, hence to
η < λ. Under that strict inequality, Proposition 4.7 gives exactly the zerofree Perron branches needed to evaluate the non-trivial determinant coordinates at z = λ−1 ; this proves (i).
If the same certificate fails, then Proposition 4.6 supplies a non-trivial
channel with spectral radius λ. Applying Theorem 4.1 to that channel
gives the boundary line field, and the proof of Proposition 4.7 shows that
the Perron-point continuation required by the non-trivial class-Mertens and
class-product constants is not available from the hypotheses of this paper.
Main Theorem I is unaffected because it is proved on the open Perron disc
and does not require evaluation at λ−1 . This proves (ii).
Finally, Corollaries 4.3 and 4.5 and proposition 4.4 all proceed by starting with the line field classified in Theorem 4.1 and then imposing finite
hypotheses that forbid that line field. No argument in this section proves
that an arbitrary broader cocycle class has no such line field. Therefore the
exported theorem chain is precisely the conditional one in (i)–(ii), with only
the stated finite boundary-obstruction eliminations as sufficient conditions.
This proves (iii).
□
Remark 4.11 (Strict-gap criteria boundary). The criteria above characterize
the boundary η = λ by a finite line-field test and provide only the hypothesis check needed for Main Theorems II–III. The loop-character classifier
Corollary 4.3 and perfect-loop-group consequence Corollary 4.5 record the
structural consequences of this boundary test that are used in the present
paper. We do not attempt here to classify all broader structural hypotheses
on the cocycle τ which force η < λ; only the finite boundary obstruction
and the perfect-loop-group elimination of that obstruction are part of the
finite-determinant product-constant theorem chain. These statements are
therefore criteria for a fixed finite-state input, not a promised classification
of cocycles with η < λ. A general theory of structural criteria for strict
twisted spectral gaps is therefore not part of the present theorem chain.
5. Quotient shadows and cyclic-detection boundary
This section proves structural consequences of the finite-group theorems
after the constants in Main Theorems II–III have already been constructed.
Quotient functoriality gives the abelianised primitive profile; the Peter–
Weyl splitting then separates the one-dimensional shadow from the higherdimensional defect. The final S3 model is a certificate for the Adams obstruction, distinguishing the quadratic sign-channel effect from the certified
standard-channel non-abelian defect. Cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle
are kept separate from the canonical cyclic-lift root-of-unity layer used later
in Section 6; neither construction is treated here as data varying over graph
resolutions or folds. None of these quotient-shadow results supplies a new
product constant; they recover and test the constants already fixed by the

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

107

Main theorem chain. Throughout this section, “boundary” means the exact linear-algebraic boundary of what cyclic quotients can detect inside the
class-function space of G.

constants is equality of the class-function expansions, hence equality of every
non-trivial Fourier coefficient, and the converse is immediate from the same
expansion.

Πτϱ (λ−1 ).

109

The equivalence of (ii) and (iii) is exactly the uniqueness of Fourier coefficients in the irreducible-character basis applied to (128): equality of the
class functions BA,τ ;G is equivalent to equality of every coefficient Πτϱ (λ−1 )
with ϱ ̸= 1. This proves (131). The final obstruction statement follows by
taking a two-element family T = {τ, τ ′ }. The scalar records agree because
they depend only on A, while the full profiles disagree by the just-proved
equivalence. This proves the claimed boundary for scalar fixed-matrix inputs.
□
Corollary 5.3 (No scalar cyclic-lift descent without a constant class profile). Fix A, G, and a non-empty family T ⊂ Tgap (A, G). Let R(A) be any
collection of fixed-matrix scalar records formed from A, for instance FP(A),
M(A), FA , the branch-lifted cyclic-lift record {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , the torsion layers {FA (ωqj )}0≤j<q , or the fixed-matrix exponential record of Definition 2.8.
These records are attached to the single matrix A, with no fold map, resolution change, or cocycle descent structure included. Then the following are
equivalent.
(i) The fixed data R(A), regarded as a candidate record for the family T,
determine a single normalised primitive class profile on T.
(ii) The class-profile map τ 7→ BA,τ ;G is constant on T.
(iii) For every non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), the Perron-point primitive channel
Πτϱ (λ−1 ) is independent of τ ∈ T.
Consequently, if two gap cocycles over the same matrix A have distinct nontrivial Perron-point primitive channels, then no fixed-matrix cyclic-lift record
of A descends to a stable cross-cocycle class-profile invariant on the two-point
family they form. Any stronger invariant requires additional descent data
beyond the fixed-matrix records of Definition 2.8.
Proof. Since R(A) is formed only from the scalar matrix A, it has the same
value for every τ ∈ T. Thus it can determine at most one candidate class
profile on T. It determines the actual normalised primitive class profile on
the whole family exactly when BA,τ ;G is independent of τ . This proves the
equivalence of (i) and (ii). The equivalence of (ii) and (iii) is the family
classification in Theorem 5.2. The final assertion follows by applying these
equivalences to T = {τ, τ ′ } and using the hypothesis that at least one nontrivial Perron-point primitive channel differs.
□

We now fix the separation between two cyclic operations. First, one may
quotient the fixed cocycle τ : EA → G. If qab : G ↠ Gab is the abelianisation
map and r : Gab ↠ Z/qZ is cyclic, the inherited cocycle r ◦ qab ◦ τ keeps the
original edge labels after reduction modulo q. Its twisted matrices depend
on the matrices of reduced edge-label counts. Second, and independently,
one may change the data by discarding the inherited labels and resetting
the target to Z/qZ with the canonical cyclic-lift cocycle τq (e) = 1 on every
edge, as in Remark 3.19. Only this reset model produces the root-of-unity
determinant channels log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 from the scalar matrix A alone.

5.3. The exact boundary of cyclic detection. The previous theorem
identifies the canonical splitting. The next statement concerns the span obtained by using all cyclic quotient observables together. It says that this
aggregate cyclic information is exactly the one-dimensional sector. An individual cyclic quotient sees only the one-dimensional characters that factor
through that quotient; here we take the span over all cyclic quotients. This
concerns quotient pullbacks on the fixed cocycle, not the canonical cyclic
lift of Section 6; no root-of-unity arithmetic for non-abelian sectors is being
asserted here.
For later use, write Ccyc (G) for the linear span of the pullbacks q ∗ f , where
q : G ↠ Z/mZ ranges over all cyclic quotients and f ranges over the class
functions on Z/mZ.
Lemma 5.9 (Cyclic quotients and one-dimensional characters). One has
Ccyc (G) = span{χ ∈ Irr(G) : dim χ = 1}.

114

HAOBO MA

Proof. Let q : G ↠ Z/mZ be a cyclic quotient and let f be a class function
on Z/mZ. Since Z/mZ is abelian, its irreducible characters form a basis of
its class functions, so

finite subgroup of the multiplicative group of complex numbers, hence cyclic.
Let qχ : G ↠ χ(G) be the induced quotient map, and let ιχ : χ(G) ,→ C×
be the tautological character. Then

so χ ∈ Ccyc (G). This proves the reverse inclusion.

Remark 5.12 (Minimal conjugation-error detector). Proposition 5.11 is precisely the counterexample described in the proof of Proposition 3.2: for
G = C3 , the correct class-indicator formula requires the conjugate (or equivalently the group inverse), and the failure without it sends every class {a} to
the wrong class {a−1 }. In the subsequent S3 example all characters happen
to be real-valued, so this failure does not manifest there; the C3 check is
therefore the minimal test that can detect a conjugation error.
5.5. The S3 obstruction witness. The preceding structural theorems
leave one finite-state question open: whether the higher-dimensional defect
can actually occur in a small symbolic skew product and whether it survives
at the product-constant level of Main Theorem III. The answer is yes: the
decisive witness is the pair of fixed-label Euler-transform values Fε (1/2) and
Fρ (1/2), with the primitive-channel intervals supplying the cyclic-detection
recovery. We record the witness here and place the routine matrix arithmetic,
primitive coefficient extraction, and rational interval checks in Appendices A
and B. The main text keeps only the rational windows needed for the theorem statement, while the appendices retain the finite rational arithmetic for
reproducibility. No step of Main Theorems I–III depends on those appendix
details; they certify the single displayed non-abelian witness. For readability,
the rational-check chain is as follows. The finite model, twisted gap, and
vanishing sign Artin logarithm are checked in Proposition A.2; the primitive fixed-label formulae are computed in Proposition A.3; the Perron-point
primitive windows are bounded in Lemma A.5, using the rational logarithm
estimates of Lemma B.1; and the product-level fixed-label windows used in
Main Theorem III are bounded in Lemma B.2.
Lemma 5.13 (Rational windows for the S3 obstruction). In the verified S3
full-shift model below, the only numerical inputs imported from the appendices are the following rational windows:
(i) −342/1000 < Πε (1/2) < −341/1000 and −719/1000 < Πρ (1/2) <
−718/1000, as stated in (159) and proved from the exact rational
truncation-and-tail bounds (171)– (174) in Lemma B.1;

116

HAOBO MA

(ii) −381/1000 < Fε (1/2) < −380/1000 and −923/1000 < Fρ (1/2) <
−922/1000, as stated in (179) and (180) and proved in Lemma B.2
by rational logarithm enclosures and rational tail bounds.
These four intervals are exact enough for the main-text obstruction because
the first pair excludes zero in the primitive sign and standard channels,
while the second pair excludes both zero and the sign-channel Artin value
Lε (1/2) = 0. No decimal approximation is used in the proof of the obstruction theorem; every endpoint above is a rational number and every strict
inequality is inherited from the cited appendix lemma.
Proof. The appendix lemma Lemma B.1 proves rational lower and upper
bounds for the finite logarithmic sums and for the remaining positive tails in
the primitive sign and standard channels. The proof of Lemma A.5 imports
precisely those bounds and records the coarser rational windows in (159);
these are item (i).
The appendix lemma Lemma B.2 applies the same rational logarithm
enclosures to the fixed-label Euler-transform sums and records the rational
windows (179) and (180); these are item (ii). Since the displayed intervals
are open rational intervals lying strictly on the negative side of zero, the
primitive windows prove non-vanishing of Πε (1/2) and Πρ (1/2), and the
fixed-label windows prove non-vanishing of Fε (1/2) and Fρ (1/2). Finally
Proposition A.2 proves Lε (z) ≡ 0, so the fixed-label sign window also proves
Fε (1/2) ̸= Lε (1/2). These are exactly the numerical hypotheses used below.
□

Therefore the mean-zero, non-scalar class-product data have precisely the
two coordinates in (138); there is no further irreducible sector in which an
additional obstruction could live. Since Lε (1/2) = 0 by Theorem 5.14, the
non-zero sign coordinate implies Fε (1/2) ̸= Lε (1/2), and the obstruction
criterion Theorem 3.66 gives a non-zero Artin-substitution error.
Finally, a class function factors through the abelianisation S3 ↠ C2 if and
only if it lies in the span of the two one-dimensional characters 1 and ε. Character orthogonality puts ρ in the orthogonal complement of that span. The
certified inequality Fρ (1/2) ̸= 0 therefore gives a genuine standard-sector
component invisible to all abelian, equivalently cyclic, quotient class observables. Hence the cyclic quotient data do not determine the full productconstant vector.
□
Corollary 5.16 (Product-constant obstruction is already finite-state). In
the certified S3 full-shift model of Theorem 5.14, the obstruction to replacing
the Adams-corrected constants of Main Theorem III by periodic Artin logarithms is completely detected by the two finite rational windows for Fε (1/2)
and Fρ (1/2). In particular, no cyclic quotient recovery can recover the product vector (KC )C⊂S3 : the sign window already forces a non-zero normalized
Artin-substitution error for some class, and the standard window lies in the
non-abelian Peter–Weyl sector orthogonal to every cyclic quotient observable.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

119

Proof. The first assertion is the fixed-label part of Theorem 5.14 together
with the determinant rigidity and obstruction criterion Theorems 3.43
and 3.66: the normalized class-product vector is the Peter–Weyl expansion
whose non-trivial coordinates are the fixed-label values Fϱ (1/2), and
equality with the naive Artin vector would force Fϱ (1/2) = Lϱ (1/2) for
every non-trivial ϱ. The certified sign interval and Lε (1/2) = 0 therefore
give a non-zero discrepancy; Corollary 3.67 converts it into the stated
classwise normalized lower bound. The second assertion follows from Theorems 5.8 and 5.10: cyclic quotient observables see only the one-dimensional
characters of S3 , while the certified non-zero Fρ (1/2) is in the standard
two-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector.
□
6. Scalar normalisation
Throughout this section, A denotes a primitive non-negative real matrix
with Perron root λ > 1. The symbols pn (A) denote the algebraic Möbius
coefficients of (1); they are primitive orbit counts only in the integer multigraph case fixed in Section 2. For the finite-group results, the scalar role
was delimited in Section 1.2: only the trivial-channel finite part M(A) is
used in Main Theorems II–III. We therefore use only the finite-part formula
and the scalar projection boundary needed for that normalization. Broader
cyclic-lift recovery and finite-observation scalar questions are separate fixedmatrix problems and are not part of the Adams–Möbius primitive-inversion
proof.

the finite-group sections; compare [5, 29, 41, 21, 32]. We keep the short
proofs to make the constants C(A), FP(A), and M(A) unambiguous.

6.2. Scalar interface for the finite-group results. The remaining scalar
material is used only as an interface for the finite-group determinant and
product results. It is not a source of non-trivial class-product coordinates:
by Theorem 3.43, those coordinates are exactly the fixed-label Peter–Weyl
Perron values. The canonical cyclic lift A⟨q⟩ = A ⊗ Pq from Equation (11),
any branch-lifted logarithmic record {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , and the root-of-unity layers of FA are fixed-matrix records. The main text uses such scalar records
only through the following boundary statement, rather than through a scalar
reconstruction theorem.

Corollary 6.5 (Only the trivial channel descends from fixed-matrix scalar
data). In the setting of Theorem 6.4, the projection of the normalised classMertens profile onto the constant class functions is exactly M(A)1G . Hence

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

123

any scalar finite-part formula or canonical cyclic-lift reconstruction that depends only on A can enter the finite-group theorem chain only through the
trivial-channel normalisation M(A). It cannot determine the non-trivial
class profile for a family T unless the non-trivial Perron-point channels
Πτϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1, are already independent of τ on that family.
Proof. The splitting (142) writes the normalised profile as the sum of the
constant class function M(A)1G and BA,τ ;G . By definition of BA,τ ;G in Definition 5.1, the latter has only non-trivial Peter–Weyl coefficients, namely the
values Πτϱ (λ−1 ) with ϱ ̸= 1. Orthogonality of irreducible characters therefore identifies the constant projection with M(A)1G and the non-constant
projection with the displayed non-trivial channels. A record depending only
on A is constant as τ varies, so it can determine this non-constant projection throughout T exactly in the independence case already characterized in
Theorem 6.4.
□
Corollary 6.6 (Rank-zero scalar obstruction for class profiles). Keep the
notation and hypotheses of Theorem 6.4. Let T ⊂ Tgap (A, G) be non-empty,
and let
Z(T) := spanR {BA,τ ;G − BA,τ ′ ;G : τ, τ ′ ∈ T}
be the real zero-mean class-function space actually swept out by the nontrivial primitive profiles in the family. Then the scalar finite part FP(A),
the scalar Mertens normalisation M(A), the reduced determinant C(A), and
every fixed-matrix cyclic-lift scalar record depending only on A have rank zero
on Z(T): they are constant on T and annihilate all profile differences.
Consequently, any finite collection of real linear invariants whose restriction to profile differences is linear and which, together with the scalar records,
recovers the normalised class-Mertens profile of Main Theorem II on T must
have real rank at least dim Z(T) on the non-trivial Peter–Weyl sector. The
same lower bound applies to any such linear recovery procedure for the product constants (KC )C⊂G of Main Theorem III after the scalar product normalisation is fixed. In particular, when the profiles in T affinely span the
whole zero-mean class-function space, at least #{conjugacy classes of G} − 1
independent non-scalar coordinates are necessary, and scalar finite-matrix
records supply none of them.
Proof. The records listed in the statement depend only on the matrix A,
not on the cocycle τ . They are therefore constant on the family T. By
Equation (142), for every τ, τ ′ ∈ T,
#
M#
A,τ ;G − MA,τ ′ ;G = BA,τ ;G − BA,τ ′ ;G .

The right-hand side is a zero-mean class function, since BA,τ ;G has only
non-trivial Peter–Weyl coefficients by Definition 5.1 and (128). Thus scalar
records see no vector in Z(T), which is the asserted rank-zero statement.
Let I be any additional finite real invariants whose restriction to profile
differences is linear and which, after adjoining the scalar records, recovers
the normalised class-Mertens profile on T. Since the scalar part is constant
on T, the induced linear difference map must distinguish every vector in
Z(T). Therefore its restriction to the real vector space Z(T) is injective,
and elementary linear algebra forces its rank to be at least dim Z(T).

124

HAOBO MA

For product constants, Theorem 3.39 and Corollary 3.44 identify the logarithmic product vector, after scalar normalisation, with the same zero-mean
Peter–Weyl boundary coordinates. Thus recovering (KC )C on T is equivalent to recovering the corresponding zero-mean profile differences, so the
same rank lower bound applies. If the affine span is the whole zero-mean
class-function space, its dimension is the number of conjugacy classes of G
minus one, because the full real class-function space has one coordinate per
conjugacy class and the scalar direction is one-dimensional.
□
The larger fixed-matrix cyclic-lift reconstruction and one-layer Fourier
inversion theory lies beyond the finite-group determinant argument. Its
role in this article is the structural warning already proved above: scalar
records identify a fixed scalar comparison model, not the non-trivial
branch-compatible class-product coordinates. The product-side finite-state
obstruction is recorded in Corollary 3.44.
7. Discussion and open problems
7.1. Summary of the main contribution. For a finite-group extension
of a mixing subshift of finite type, the twisted determinant family records
periodic labels. The main point of this paper is that the corresponding
primitive labels are recovered by a finite Adams–Möbius calculation in Peter–
Weyl coordinates. This is the finite-state correction forced by the repetition
rule g 7→ g m for primitive orbits. Under the twisted gap η < λ, the recovered
primitive channels have Perron-point values and hence determine the classindexed constants MC (A, τ ; G) in the Frobenius-class Mertens sums.
The Euler-product constants require the same distinction. Once the classical leading exponent |C|/|G| has been separated, the non-trivial coordinates
of the constant are the fixed-label Euler transform values Fϱ (λ−1 ), not the
periodic Artin logarithms Lϱ (λ−1 ). The determinant formula therefore gives






X
|C| 
γ + log C(A) +
χ∗ϱ (C)Fϱ (λ−1 ) ,
KC = exp−
|G|
ϱ̸=1

with the positive real branch (Corollaries 3.10 and 3.62, theorems 3.33, 3.39
and 3.72, and proposition 3.58). This is a determinant-level refinement of
the classical Chebotarev/Mertens orbit-counting picture, rather than a new
leading-density theorem. Quotient functoriality identifies the abelianised
primitive profile, and cyclic quotient observables span precisely the onedimensional Peter–Weyl shadow. The S3 example shows both phenomena
concretely: the sign line detects an Adams correction invisible to the Artin
logarithm, while the standard two-dimensional representation gives a genuine non-abelian component.
The scalar finite-part and cyclic-lift calculations supply only the trivialchannel normalisation and a fixed-matrix comparison model. The projection
theorem Theorem 5.2 marks the boundary: after normalising the class constants, scalar data give the constant class-function component M(A)1G ,
whereas the non-trivial primitive profile is the Peter–Weyl vector BA,τ ;G .

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

125

7.2. Further directions. Several natural extensions require hypotheses different from the finite-dimensional theorem chain proved here. A
Hölder/Ruelle transfer-operator version would require Banach-space
hypotheses and determinant convention comparisons not used in the
finite-matrix proof. Refined secondary spectral-edge terms beyond the
Perron-boundary constants require their own separation hypotheses; here
we use only the noninterference statement proved in Proposition 3.64. Broad
sufficient criteria for the strict twisted gap would be a cocycle-classification
problem, whereas the present eta-gap material is a finite Perron-point
test and boundary obstruction. Fixed-matrix cyclic-lift reconstruction,
scalar-boundary finite-observation limits, and inverse problems from partial
class observations are separate directions, not suppressed hypotheses for
the present results.
The appendix material follows the same convention. This article retains
the scalar normalisation and finite reproducibility records needed by the
finite-group determinant constants: the scalar constant C(A), the finite part
FP(A), the no-descent warning, the fixed-label rational enclosures, and the
explicit S3 obstruction calculation. The arguments above use only the finite interfaces proved here: the eta-gap criterion, the robustness statement
Proposition 3.64, the fixed-label records, and the fixed-matrix scalar boundary theorem. Further cyclic-lift, finite-observation, secondary-asymptotic,
and universality statements require additional theorems not assumed in this
paper.

resulting rational windows. The opening quadratic Adams obstruction is
intentionally a proposition, not a theorem-level main result; all references
use the proposition label accordingly.

Remark B.3 (Exact-arithmetic reproducibility). The mathematical verification in this appendix is the displayed rational arithmetic in Lemmas B.1
and B.2. A reader or auxiliary exact-arithmetic program can reproduce the
calculation by computing the rational logarithm endpoints L3 (q) ≤ log q ≤
U3 (q), multiplying them by the signed rational coefficients in E7 , R15 , Q8 ,
and S32 , adding the four rational tails, and clearing positive denominators
in the four final interval comparisons. The auxiliary files with basename
s3_log_certificates in the certificates/ directory are optional serializations of this exact-arithmetic transcript, including the script, certificate,
output, and run note. They are not additional mathematical inputs for the
appendix.
Appendix C. Appendix: local finite reproducibility records
for the S3 witness
This appendix records the finite rational reproducibility format used
by Proposition 3.60. It supports the determinant evaluation and S3
Adams-obstruction witness in Main Theorem III by making the relevant
finite constants checkable by rational inequalities. The main text uses only
the finite determinant computability theorem, the determinant-to-product
Fourier map, and the stated finite windows.
Proposition C.1 (Finite rational reproducibility record). Assume the hypotheses of Theorem 3.33. Fix a non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), a tolerance ϵ > 0,
and a truncation level M satisfying (91). The output of Proposition 3.60
is reproduced by the following finite data. The record consists only of finite
algebraic model data and rational inequalities of the following four kinds:
(i) the list of triples (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M , with the scalar Perron triple
(1, 1, 1) deleted;
(m)
(ii) the Adams coefficients aϱ,π = ⟨ψ m χϱ , χπ ⟩G , written from the finite
character table and class-power table;
(iii) for every listed triple, a branch tag specifying either the Abel non-trivial
Perron branch (rm = 1, π ̸= 1) or the ordinary germ value inside the
Perron disc (rm ≥ 2), together with a rational rectangular interval
enclosure for Log det(I − λ−rm Aπ )−1 ;
(iv) two rational error bounds RM and Rnum : the tail bound RM is certified
either directly from (93) or from the rational envelope (94), and Rnum
is certified from the finite interval-propagation bound (95).
Any checker which verifies these four records by exact algebraic comparison and rational interval arithmetic produces a rational rectangular interval
(M )
IM ⊂ C enclosing the finite sum Fϱ
and certifies the inclusion
(181)

Fϱ (λ−1 ) ∈ IM + {w ∈ C : |w| ≤ RM + Rnum } ,

where all sides of IM and both radii are rational. Thus the displayed finite record gives an exact reproducibility certificate for the constant used in
Proposition 3.60 and corollary 3.62.
Proof. The first two records are finite because the set {(r, m) : rm ≤ M }
and Irr(G) are finite, and because the Adams coefficients are finite charactertable inner products. The deleted scalar triple is exactly the omitted term

138

HAOBO MA

in Proposition 3.60; its coefficient is zero for ϱ ̸= 1, so no certificate record
must assign a finite value to the singular scalar logarithm.
For each remaining triple, the branch tag is one of the two cases already
proved in item (d) of Proposition 3.60: either the non-trivial Abel branch at
rm = 1, made regular by the strict twisted gap, or the ordinary logarithmic
germ at rm ≥ 2, where ρ(λ−rm Aπ ) < 1. Thus every tagged interval encloses
a uniquely specified logarithm. Multiplying the rational rectangular inter(m)
vals by the certified coefficients µ(m)aϱ,π /(rm), enclosing each algebraic
product by exact comparison in a rational rectangle, and summing those
rectangles gives a rational rectangular interval IM which contains the stated
finite truncation. Its excess radius is at most Rnum by the triangle inequality
used in (95). The discarded terms with rm > M have total absolute value at
most the rational number RM , either because the certificate compares RM
with the algebraic tail in (93), or because it records rational θ, Qϱ satisfying
(94) and RM ≥ Qϱ θM +1 /(1 − θ). All these checks are finite algebraic or rational inequalities. Adding the two independent radii proves (181). Finally,
Corollary 3.62 applies finite Fourier inversion to the certified fixed-label Perron vector, so the same certificate record serializes the product constants
rather than introducing a new analytic premise.
□
Proposition C.2 (Certificate normal form for fixed-label product recovery).
Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.39, every finite reproducibility record
accepted by the fixed-label algorithm and used for the Main Theorem III
product constants consists, for each non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), exactly of the
following finite fields:
(i) the determinant model (Dπ (z))π∈Irr(G) , the Perron root λ, the twisted
gap certificate η < λ, the character table, and the class-power maps;
(ii) rational tail data Cϱ , Qϱ , θ, M satisfying the majorant hypothesis of
Lemma 3.14, λ−1 < θ < 1, Cϱ λ < Qϱ , and (90);
(iii) the finite window of all triples (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M , with only
(m)
(1, 1, 1) deleted, and all Adams coefficients aϱ,π in that window;
(iv) for every non-deleted listed triple, a branch tag and a rational rectangular enclosure for Log det(I − λ−rm Aπ )−1 , using the Abel tag for
(rm = 1, π ̸= 1) and the ordinary Perron-disc tag for rm ≥ 2;
(v) the rational finite-propagation radius obtained from (95), and the rational tail radius RM,ϱ = Qϱ θM +1 /(1 − θ).
The checker which verifies these fields returns a disc
Iϱ + {w ∈ C : |w| ≤ RM,ϱ + Rnum,ϱ }
containing Fϱ (λ−1 ), where Iϱ is the interval evaluation of the finite sum (92).
Conversely, every accepted finite enclosure produced by Proposition 3.60 is
equivalent, after reordering the finite window and splitting the total error
into its tail and finite-propagation parts, to such a five-field record. This
necessity is only a normal form for the stated fixed-label algorithm and its
accepted records; it is not a claim that no other certificate architecture could
prove the same numerical inclusions. Applying these records for all nontrivial ϱ and then the Fourier formula (97) gives rational enclosures for all
Frobenius-class product constants (KC )C . Thus the appendix certificates,

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

including any optional exact-arithmetic transcripts under certificates/,
are serializations of this finite record and are not additional computational
hypotheses.
Proof. The model field is finite because the group has finitely many irreducible representations and conjugacy classes, and because each twisted
determinant is a finite polynomial. The tail field is finite and checkable
by algebraic comparison: Cϱ is a certified majorant from Lemma 3.14, the
inequalities for Qϱ and θ are rational/algebraic comparisons, and (90) is a
rational inequality. By Proposition 3.60, this tail field gives the analytic
error bound (93) through the rational envelope (94).
For the finite window, the Adams coefficients are recomputed from the
finite character-table inner product (89). The branch field is exactly the
two-branch convention of Proposition 3.60: the strict twisted gap makes
every non-trivial rm = 1 Abel determinant regular at the boundary, while
rm ≥ 2 lies inside the Perron disc. Signed interval summation over this
finite window gives Iϱ . Enlarging by the finite-propagation radius of (95)
and by RM,ϱ contains the exact value by the triangle inequality and by the
tail estimate already proved. This proves the checker soundness.
Conversely, the output of Proposition 3.60 has no further analytic datum:
it uses the same determinant model, the same Adams coefficients, the same
deleted-triple and branch convention, a finite truncation level, finitely many
determinant-logarithm enclosures, and the sum of one tail error and one
finite interval-propagation error. Recording those entries in the five fields
above, and replacing an exact tail comparison by any rational envelope satisfying (90), gives the same certified disc. The converse is therefore an internal
normal-form statement for accepted outputs of that finite algorithm, not a
universal optimality assertion about all possible certification methods. Finally, Corollary 3.62 expresses the normalized product logarithms by the
finite character-table Fourier transform (97); exponentiating the resulting
real intervals gives the stated enclosures for the positive constants KC . □
Corollary C.3 (Fixed-label product certificate audit). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.39, every finite product-constant certificate accepted by the
fixed-label algorithm has exactly the five-field normal form of Proposition C.2.
Conversely, any accepted five-field normal-form record gives rational enclosures for all positive Frobenius-class product constants (KC )C . The word
“exactly” refers only to this accepted-record normal form for the stated algorithm.
Proof. This is the soundness and converse assertion of Proposition C.2: the
determinant model, rational tail data, finite triple window, branch-tagged
logarithm enclosures, and the two error radii are precisely the data used by
Proposition 3.60. The final passage from the fixed-label Perron enclosures to
product-constant enclosures is the finite character-table Fourier transform
(97), followed by exponentiation on the positive branch of Theorem 3.39. □

Proof. The sufficiency direction is exactly the finite truncation theorem in
compressed form. Items (i) and (ii) are finite by the finiteness of Irr(G),
the finite character table, and the finite set {(r, m) : rm ≤ M }. The scalar

by the twisted spectral gap, while all terms with rm ≥ 2 lie strictly inside
the Perron disc. Summing the certified finite rectangles with coefficients

computation in Theorem 3.43 and corollary 3.62.

A checker for these fields returns exactly the interval inclusion (181). Conversely, every finite certificate record used by Proposition 3.60 determines
the five fields above after harmless reordering of the finite window. Thus the
finite exact-arithmetic transcripts, if supplied separately, are only serializations of the local S3 verification, not new analytic assumptions or a general
verification theory.
Proof. Starting from the four records in Proposition 3.60, put the base matrix, Perron algebraic number, group table, class table, character table, and
class-power table in the model field. The window field is the finite triple
list in record (i) together with M and ϵ. The Adams field is record (ii).
The branch field is record (iii). The bound field is record (iv). Therefore
the schema contains no datum beyond the finite determinant reproducibility
proposition.
The verifier checks listed in the last column are precisely the proof steps
of Proposition 3.60: finite character orthogonality recomputes the Adams
coefficients; the deleted scalar triple is the unique singular Perron logarithm
and has zero coefficient for ϱ ̸= 1; the two branch tags are the two regularity
(m)
cases in Proposition 3.60; interval multiplication by µ(m)aϱ,π /(rm) and
finite summation give radius Rnum ; and the discarded triples with rm > M
contribute at most RM . Hence acceptance gives (181).
Conversely, any four-record certificate of the kind used here already specifies the same model data, finite window, Adams coefficients, branch intervals, and two radii; ordering the finite triple list lexicographically is the only
normalization needed to place it in the displayed fields. A concrete S3 transcript instantiates these fields with exact rational arithmetic, so its role is
